### On Fitting Flow Models with Large Sinkhorn Couplings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05526v1)
- **Authors**: Michal Klein, Alireza Mousavi-Hosseini, Stephen Zhang, Marco Cuturi
- **Abstract**: Flow models transform data gradually from one modality (e.g. noise) onto another (e.g. images). Such models are parameterized by a time-dependent velocity field, trained to fit segments connecting pairs of source and target points. When the pairing between source and target points is given, training flow models boils down to a supervised regression problem. When no such pairing exists, as is the case when generating data from noise, training flows is much harder. A popular approach lies in picking source and target points independently. This can, however, lead to velocity fields that are slow to train, but also costly to integrate at inference time. In theory, one would greatly benefit from training flow models by sampling pairs from an optimal transport (OT) measure coupling source and target, since this would lead to a highly efficient flow solving the Benamou and Brenier dynamical OT problem. In practice, recent works have proposed to sample mini-batches of $n$ source and $n$ target points and reorder them using an OT solver to form better pairs. These works have advocated using batches of size $n\approx 256$, and considered OT solvers that return couplings that are either sharp (using e.g. the Hungarian algorithm) or blurred (using e.g. entropic regularization, a.k.a. Sinkhorn). We follow in the footsteps of these works by exploring the benefits of increasing $n$ by three to four orders of magnitude, and look more carefully on the effect of the entropic regularization $\varepsilon$ used in the Sinkhorn algorithm. Our analysis is facilitated by new scale invariant quantities to report the sharpness of a coupling, while our sharded computations across multiple GPU or GPU nodes allow scaling up $n$. We show that in both synthetic and image generation tasks, flow models greatly benefit when fitted with large Sinkhorn couplings, with a low entropic regularization $\varepsilon$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the benefits of using large mini-batch sizes with the Sinkhorn algorithm within the framework of flow matching models for generative tasks.  Flow matching involves training a time-dependent velocity field to transform one probability distribution (e.g., noise) into another (e.g., images). Recent work has explored using optimal transport (OT) to better pair source and target points in mini-batches. This paper extends these approaches by significantly increasing the mini-batch size (up to 2 million points) and thoroughly exploring the impact of entropic regularization (ε) within the Sinkhorn algorithm. The authors introduce scale-invariant metrics to assess the sharpness of the OT coupling. Their experiments, on synthetic and image generation tasks, suggest that fitting flow models with large Sinkhorn couplings, and carefully chosen regularization, leads to improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the scale of the Sinkhorn computations performed and the systematic exploration of its effects on flow matching. Previous works were limited by the computational cost of OT solvers, restricting mini-batch sizes. Scaling to millions of points and providing scale-invariant regularization tuning is a significant computational contribution. The authors also modify the Sinkhorn algorithm for greater stability by removing the norms and focusing exclusively on the dot product.

*   **Significance:** The results have the potential to influence how flow matching models are trained. The paper demonstrates that a key limitation in the current approach, the small batch sizes in OT-based Flow Matching, can be overcome through efficient implementations of Sinkhorn allowing for better optimization of flow models. The study is particularly relevant for situations where inference speed is crucial, and more efficient training is valued. The introduction of a scale-free metric of sharpness for couplings contributes methodologically.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Computational Contribution:**  The scaling of Sinkhorn to millions of points is a significant engineering feat, enabling the investigation of new regimes for OT-based flow matching.
    *   **Systematic Study:** The paper provides a comprehensive exploration of mini-batch size and entropic regularization, providing practical guidelines for practitioners.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are carried out on a range of synthetic and real-world datasets.
    *   **Stable Implementation:** The removal of the norms from Sinkhorn distances increases implementation stability.
    *   **Analysis:** Clear metrics provided for the analysis of couplings in the context of flow matching, contributing to the understanding of the role of OT within these frameworks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Advance:** The paper builds directly on existing work in flow matching and OT. The advances, while important, are primarily focused on scaling and refined analysis rather than fundamentally new algorithmic ideas.
    *   **Computational Cost Concerns:** Although the paper focuses on inference efficiency, the initial computational overhead to produce the coupling is not fully addressed. The improvements depend on a preprocessing step that may prove impractical in resource constrained environments.
    *   **Limited theoretical analysis**: the results are purely empirical and, although providing scale-invariant measures for coupling analysis, lacks in theoretical insights or guarantees.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper may encourage practitioners to explore larger batch sizes in flow matching. Additionally, the methodological contributions may influence the benchmarking of future approaches.

*   **Rigorous Rationale**:
The score reflects the solid contributions made in scaling and analyzing Sinkhorn with flow matching. While the overall algorithmic concept is not revolutionary, the authors show convincingly that large mini-batch sizes and careful regularization can improve flow model training and performance.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sequence Modeling for N-Agent Ad Hoc Teamwork
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05527v1)
- **Authors**: Caroline Wang, Di Yang Shi, Elad Liebman, Ishan Durugkar, Arrasy Rahman, Peter Stone
- **Abstract**: N-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT) is a newly introduced challenge in multi-agent reinforcement learning, where controlled subteams of varying sizes must dynamically collaborate with varying numbers and types of unknown teammates without pre-coordination. The existing learning algorithm (POAM) considers only independent learning for its flexibility in dealing with a changing number of agents. However, independent learning fails to fully capture the inter-agent dynamics essential for effective collaboration. Based on our observation that transformers deal effectively with sequences with varying lengths and have been shown to be highly effective for a variety of machine learning problems, this work introduces a centralized, transformer-based method for N-agent ad hoc teamwork. Our proposed approach incorporates historical observations and actions of all controlled agents, enabling optimal responses to diverse and unseen teammates in partially observable environments. Empirical evaluation on a StarCraft II task demonstrates that MAT-NAHT outperforms POAM, achieving superior sample efficiency and generalization, without auxiliary agent-modeling objectives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new method, MAT-NAHT (Multi-Agent Transformer for N-Agent Ad Hoc Teamwork), to address the challenge of N-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT). NAHT involves controlled teams of varying sizes collaborating with dynamically changing numbers of unknown teammates without pre-coordination. MAT-NAHT leverages a centralized, transformer-based approach, processing the historical observations and actions of controlled agents to enable optimal responses to diverse and unseen teammates. The method is evaluated on the StarCraft II SMAC environment, demonstrating superior sample efficiency and generalization compared to the existing POAM algorithm, without relying on explicit agent modeling objectives.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting a transformer architecture, particularly a centralized one similar to MAT, to the NAHT problem. While transformers have been used in MARL before (e.g., MAT), this paper applies it to the NAHT setting, which has its own unique challenges related to varying team sizes and unknown teammates. Centralized approaches have become increasingly attractive in MARL, so applying it to NAHT is a sensible and incremental advancement. Using historical data (actions and observations) within the transformer also addresses a crucial limitation in prior work. While the *application* is novel, the *core techniques* are not groundbreaking. It builds upon existing work. It could be argued that combining a specific network architecture with a novel framing of a problem is a typical publication.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is that it provides a more effective solution (MAT-NAHT) for NAHT than the existing POAM method. Showing improved sample efficiency and generalization are important contributions. This demonstrates the potential of transformers for capturing inter-agent dynamics in the NAHT setting. The results are encouraging, suggesting that centralized approaches *can* be effective in handling the challenges of NAHT. That being said, NAHT itself is a relatively new problem, and its long-term impact is still unknown.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined algorithm (MAT-NAHT).
    *   Empirical validation on a challenging benchmark (SMAC).
    *   Demonstrated improvement over the baseline (POAM).
    * Addresses a practical problem that is becoming increasingly important
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental improvement. The core building blocks (transformer, centralized learning) are not fundamentally new. The results, while positive, aren't extraordinarily impactful.
    *   Limited scope of evaluation. Only one SMAC task (3s vs 5z) is used. Testing across a wider range of tasks would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   Lack of deeper analysis. While performance gains are shown, the paper does not deeply analyze *why* MAT-NAHT performs better. Does it learn better agent models implicitly? What specific aspect of the transformer architecture is most crucial? The conclusions in the "Discussion" section are rather weak in this respect.
    * POAM, the primary baseline, is not considered a particularly high bar in the current landscape.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper provides a solid contribution to the emerging field of NAHT by adapting a transformer architecture to address its specific challenges. While the novelty is incremental (primarily applying known techniques to a new problem setting), the empirical results demonstrate a clear improvement over the existing baseline. However, the limited evaluation scope and the lack of deeper analysis limit the overall impact of the paper. Its true impact will depend on how much future work builds on the approach for NAHT.

### Spectral Graph Neural Networks are Incomplete on Graphs with a Simple Spectrum
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05530v1)
- **Authors**: Snir Hordan, Maya Bechler-Speicher, Gur Lifshitz, Nadav Dym
- **Abstract**: Spectral features are widely incorporated within Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to improve their expressive power, or their ability to distinguish among non-isomorphic graphs. One popular example is the usage of graph Laplacian eigenvectors for positional encoding in MPNNs and Graph Transformers. The expressive power of such Spectrally-enhanced GNNs (SGNNs) is usually evaluated via the k-WL graph isomorphism test hierarchy and homomorphism counting. Yet, these frameworks align poorly with the graph spectra, yielding limited insight into SGNNs' expressive power. We leverage a well-studied paradigm of classifying graphs by their largest eigenvalue multiplicity to introduce an expressivity hierarchy for SGNNs. We then prove that many SGNNs are incomplete even on graphs with distinct eigenvalues. To mitigate this deficiency, we adapt rotation equivariant neural networks to the graph spectra setting to propose a method to provably improve SGNNs' expressivity on simple spectrum graphs. We empirically verify our theoretical claims via an image classification experiment on the MNIST Superpixel dataset and eigenvector canonicalization on graphs from ZINC.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the expressive power of Spectral Graph Neural Networks (SGNNs), specifically focusing on Eigenspace Projection GNNs (EPNNs). The authors demonstrate that many SGNNs, including EPNNs, are incomplete even when operating on graphs with a simple spectrum (i.e., distinct eigenvalues). They show that EPNN cannot distinguish between certain non-isomorphic graphs with distinct eigenvalues.  To address this, the paper introduces "equiEPNN," a modified EPNN architecture inspired by equivariant neural networks for point clouds, which incorporates Euclidean message passing to improve expressiveness. The authors provide theoretical guarantees for completeness under certain sparsity conditions of eigenvectors and demonstrate empirically that equiEPNN can outperform EPNN in image classification (MNIST Superpixel) and eigenvector canonicalization (ZINC).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting a previously unaddressed limitation of SGNNs. While spectral methods have been incorporated into GNNs for some time, a formal analysis of their expressiveness limitations *specifically* concerning spectral properties like eigenvalue multiplicity is relatively novel. The introduction of equiEPNN, inspired by equivariant point cloud networks, provides a practical solution to the identified limitation, adding to the paper's novelty. However, the connection to point cloud networks, while useful, does somewhat diminish the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by promoting more thoughtful integration of spectral information in GNNs. By demonstrating the incompleteness of EPNN, the paper encourages researchers to move beyond simplistic approaches and consider the underlying spectral properties of graphs when designing their architectures. The paper offers theoretical guarantees that are significant to understanding when SGNNs will perform well.  While limited to graphs with simple spectrum, this result has potentially important ramifications. The empirical evaluations offer practical validation and guidance for future work.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides a strong theoretical foundation, clearly defining the problem and presenting proofs for its claims.
    *   **Practical Solution:**  The introduction of equiEPNN offers a practical and relatively simple solution to the identified limitations.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on MNIST Superpixel and ZINC provide empirical support for the theoretical claims.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope of Results:** The theoretical results are primarily focused on graphs with a simple spectrum. While this simplification is useful for analysis, it limits the direct applicability of the results to more complex graphs commonly encountered in real-world applications. The graphs studied are also limited to simple spectrum.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While equiEPNN improves expressiveness, its performance gains over EPNN may be somewhat marginal in certain settings. The BREC expressivity experiments are inconclusive.
    *   **Limited Generalization:**  The reliance on certain sparsity patterns in eigenvectors to guarantee completeness limits how generally this can be applied.
    *   **Computational Complexity:** While the authors mention that they are increasing the expressive power of EPNN without significantly increasing model complexity, they provide very little detail on this claim.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in SGNNs by promoting more careful consideration of spectral properties and encouraging the development of more expressive and robust architectures. It provides a clear framework for analyzing the expressiveness of SGNNs based on spectral graph theory, which can guide future research.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**  The paper is well-written, theoretically sound, and empirically validated. The identification of the incompleteness of SGNNs on graphs with a simple spectrum represents a meaningful contribution. The proposed equiEPNN offers a practical solution.  However, the scope of the theoretical analysis is somewhat limited (simple spectrum graphs), and the empirical gains of equiEPNN, while present, are not overwhelmingly large. Although the results of the theoretical analysis may be difficult to extend to graphs with multiple eigenvalues, they still have important ramifications for the field. Overall, the paper makes an important contribution to the theory and practice of SGNNs. However, the points above diminish the paper's overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Using Large Language Models to Simulate Human Behavioural Experiments: Port of Mars
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05555v1)
- **Authors**: Oliver Slumbers, Joel Z. Leibo, Marco A. Janssen
- **Abstract**: Collective risk social dilemmas (CRSD) highlight a trade-off between individual preferences and the need for all to contribute toward achieving a group objective. Problems such as climate change are in this category, and so it is critical to understand their social underpinnings. However, rigorous CRSD methodology often demands large-scale human experiments but it is difficult to guarantee sufficient power and heterogeneity over socio-demographic factors. Generative AI offers a potential complementary approach to address thisproblem. By replacing human participants with large language models (LLM), it allows for a scalable empirical framework. This paper focuses on the validity of this approach and whether it is feasible to represent a large-scale human-like experiment with sufficient diversity using LLM. In particular, where previous literature has focused on political surveys, virtual towns and classical game-theoretic examples, we focus on a complex CRSD used in the institutional economics and sustainability literature known as Port of Mars
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the feasibility and validity of using Large Language Models (LLMs) to simulate human behavior in Collective Risk Social Dilemmas (CRSDs), specifically using the "Port of Mars" (PoM) game. The authors aim to create a scalable empirical framework by replacing human participants with LLM agents, allowing for more extensive and diverse experimentation than traditional methods. They focus on assessing the algorithmic fidelity of LLM agent responses and demonstrate the flexibility of this approach by modeling interactions between agents with different Social Value Orientations (SVOs) and investigating the impact of leadership. The study evaluates forward continuity and pattern correspondence, comparing LLM-generated data with existing human data from PoM experiments. The authors find initial signs of algorithmic fidelity and demonstrate that LLMs can simulate the results of human behavior experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of LLMs to a complex CRSD (Port of Mars) beyond simple game-theoretic scenarios. While previous work has explored LLMs in political surveys, virtual towns, and classical game examples, this paper focuses on a more nuanced CRSD with temporal extendedness, partial information, and uncertainty. The choice of PoM, a relatively novel game not heavily represented in LLM training data, is a strength. However, the central idea of using LLMs to simulate human behaviour in social dilemmas is not entirely new, as demonstrated by work like generative agents (Park et al., 2023).

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is substantial. If LLMs can effectively simulate human behavior in CRSDs, it opens avenues for faster, cheaper, and more scalable research in sustainability, institutional economics, and other fields. The ability to model diverse populations and test various scenarios could lead to better understanding and mitigation strategies for real-world problems like climate change. However, the paper emphasizes that this is a complementary approach, and LLMs will not replace real human experiments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Focus on Validity:** The paper explicitly addresses the critical question of algorithmic fidelity, adapting criteria from previous research.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The prompt engineering and experimental setup are clearly explained, enabling replicability.
    *   **Demonstration of Flexibility:** The experiments on SVOs and leadership showcase the model's capacity to simulate complex social dynamics.
    *   **Acknowledgement of Limitations:** The authors are upfront about the limitations of their work, including the partial validation of algorithmic fidelity and the reliance on a single LLM.
    *   **Well-chosen experimental platform:** Using a novel, less-exposed CRSD such as Port of Mars avoids concerns about the LLMs simply regurgitating existing strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Algorithmic Fidelity Validation:** Only two out of the four conditions of algorithmic fidelity (forward continuity and pattern correspondence) were evaluated due to the lack of available human data.  This significantly weakens the claims of validity.
    *   **Reliance on a Single LLM:** The experiments were conducted using only Gemini-1.5-flash-002, limiting the generalizability of the findings. Testing across different LLMs is essential.
    *   **Survival rates higher than human data**: The very high survival rates, significantly higher than those observed in human studies, suggest that LLMs may exhibit inherent biases or different decision-making processes.
    *   **Discrepancies with human experiments**: Certain metrics and patterns, most notably on the community group, differed when compared to the human data and rankings.
    *   **Cheap Talk interpretation of leader behaviours**: While there are reasonable explanations for the observations regarding leadership behaviours, such interpretations need to be validated with stronger tests.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the challenges of algorithmic fidelity can be addressed, this approach could significantly influence research methodologies in several fields.

*   **Justification for Score:**

While the paper tackles an important problem and demonstrates a promising approach, the limited validation of algorithmic fidelity and reliance on a single LLM are significant weaknesses.  The discrepancies between the generated and original data need to be explained and addressed. While it is an initial investigation, the degree to which the LLM simulates human behaviour remains unclear. The lack of a comparison across various LLMs makes the paper difficult to make any conclusions on whether human behaviour can be replicated on these frameworks. With additional validation and replication across models, this research could be significant. Therefore, a more moderate score is justified.

### Improving LLMs with a knowledge from databases
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05560v1)
- **Authors**: Petr Máša
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are achieving significant progress almost every moment now. Many advanced techniques have been introduced and widely accepted, like retrieval-augmentation generation (RAG), agents, and tools. Tools can query the database to answer questions from structured data files or perform groupings or other statistics. This unlocks huge opportunities, such as it can answer any question, but also poses threats, such as safety, because there is no control over the commands that are created. We would like to discuss whether we can create a new method that improves answers based on dataset/database via some interpretable ML methods, namely enhanced association rules. The advantage would be if the method can be also used in some safe technique like RAG. Association rules have a sound history. Since the introduction of CN2 and aproiri, many enhancements have been made. In parallel, enhanced association rules have been introduced and evolved over the last 40 years. The general problem is typically that there are too many rules. There are some techniques for handling it, but when LLM emerged, it turned out to be the best use case for the RAG technique for LLMs. We proposed a method that generates a ruleset based on defined knowledge patterns, then converts rules into text form via a rule-to-text converter, and includes the result as an RAG into LLM. We compared this method with ChatGPT (even with using agents) and we have discovered a significant improvement in answering questions based on the dataset. We have also tried several strategies how much rules to generate. We found this improvement interesting. Moreover, it can also be improved in many ways as future work, like incorporating other patterns, the use of rule mining as an agent, and many others.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on its novelty and significance, followed by a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Improving LLMs with Knowledge from Databases" explores a method to enhance Large Language Models (LLMs) by integrating knowledge discovered from databases using enhanced association rules. The core idea is to leverage the interpretability of enhanced association rules to extract patterns and relationships within a dataset. These rules are then converted into natural language sentences and incorporated into the LLM's context via Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). The authors compare this approach against using ChatGPT directly (with and without agents) and a smaller LLM model without agents and with RAG, demonstrating improvements in the quality of the answers when using the proposed method. The paper focuses on analyzing a traffic accident dataset and answering the question: "On which circumstances occur fatal accidents more than usual?". The authors mine knowledge patterns from database, converts results to text, and includes them in RAG. They found an improvement in answering questions based on the dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Incremental:** The paper builds upon existing techniques such as RAG and association rule mining. The combination of these isn't entirely new, as using external knowledge with LLMs is a well-established area. However, the specific application of *enhanced* association rules and the explicit conversion to natural language for RAG offers a slight variation. The use of enhanced association rules over the traditional ones is a good idea due to their advanced knowledge capabilities.

    *   **Limited Methodological Innovation:** The approach to converting rules into sentences seems rather straightforward and could benefit from more sophisticated natural language generation techniques. The 'Rule-to-Text' module, a key component, isn't described in enough technical detail, making it difficult to assess its sophistication.
*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The problem addressed—improving LLM performance on domain-specific data—is highly relevant. The ability to leverage structured databases and extract meaningful insights automatically can be valuable in many applications, specially in business and companies databases.

    *   **Limited Empirical Evidence:** The evaluation is based on a single dataset and a qualitative comparison. While the results suggest improvements, more rigorous quantitative metrics and comparisons against other knowledge injection methods are needed to demonstrate significant advantages. It would be better to use also other datasets and other analytical approaches. Note that they said they have found no benchmarks suitable, but they could create it.

    *   **Scalability and Generalizability Concerns:** The paper doesn't extensively discuss the scalability of the approach. Mining and processing enhanced association rules can be computationally expensive for large databases. Moreover, the method's performance might depend on the nature of the data and the chosen patterns, warranting further investigation.

    *   **Lack of Ablation studies**: As an important detail, the paper lacks ablation studies. The authors found the improvements using RAG, but they do not demonstrate if those answers are a direct consequence of using enhanced association rules or the use of other knowledge-based techniques might provide similar results.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Interpretability:** A key strength is the focus on using interpretable ML methods. This allows users to understand the reasoning behind the LLM's answers, which is crucial for trust and transparency.

    *   **Safe approach**: The use of RAG does not include risks of harmful SQL code generation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The qualitative assessment and reliance on only one dataset weaken the conclusions.

    *   **Lack of Technical Depth:** Some parts of the methodology (especially the Rule-to-Text conversion) lack sufficient technical details.

    *   **Scalability and Generalizability**: No data and discussion for them.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a promising approach to enhance LLMs with knowledge extracted from databases, leveraging the interpretability of enhanced association rules. However, the limited methodological innovation, qualitative evaluation, and lack of detailed technical explanations warrant a more conservative assessment. Therefore, its novelty is limited. The impact is a bit higher, but the article is too focused on the UK accident dataset and the results lacks in generalizability. The score is significantly affected by lack of ablation studies, as authors might have found similar results when using other ML approaches to the dataset.

### ScaleRTL: Scaling LLMs with Reasoning Data and Test-Time Compute for Accurate RTL Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05566v1)
- **Authors**: Chenhui Deng, Yun-Da Tsai, Guan-Ting Liu, Zhongzhi Yu, Haoxing Ren
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled near-human performance on software coding benchmarks, but their effectiveness in RTL code generation remains limited due to the scarcity of high-quality training data. While prior efforts have fine-tuned LLMs for RTL tasks, they do not fundamentally overcome the data bottleneck and lack support for test-time scaling due to their non-reasoning nature. In this work, we introduce ScaleRTL, the first reasoning LLM for RTL coding that scales up both high-quality reasoning data and test-time compute. Specifically, we curate a diverse set of long chain-of-thought reasoning traces averaging 56K tokens each, resulting in a dataset of 3.5B tokens that captures rich RTL knowledge. Fine-tuning a general-purpose reasoning model on this corpus yields ScaleRTL that is capable of deep RTL reasoning. Subsequently, we further enhance the performance of ScaleRTL through a novel test-time scaling strategy that extends the reasoning process via iteratively reflecting on and self-correcting previous reasoning steps. Experimental results show that ScaleRTL achieves state-of-the-art performance on VerilogEval and RTLLM, outperforming 18 competitive baselines by up to 18.4% on VerilogEval and 12.7% on RTLLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a rigorous critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ScaleRTL: Scaling LLMs with Reasoning Data and Test-Time Compute for Accurate RTL Code Generation" introduces ScaleRTL, a novel approach to improving LLM performance in RTL (Register-Transfer Level) code generation. The approach focuses on scaling both the training data and the test-time compute. First, it curates a large dataset of RTL code paired with detailed chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning traces by using DeepSeek-R1 to generate long, detailed explanations for RTL scripts, creating a 3.5 billion token corpus. Then, it fine-tunes the DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen model on this dataset to create ScaleRTL. At inference time, the authors introduce a novel iterative self-correction mechanism, prompting the model to rethink and refine its reasoning process when an initial attempt fails, effectively scaling test-time compute. Experiments on VerilogEval and RTLLM benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, outperforming existing LLMs and specialized RTL coding models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in applying LLMs to RTL code generation: the scarcity of high-quality training data and the lack of reasoning capabilities. The novelty lies in a two-pronged approach:  (1) generating a large-scale reasoning dataset for RTL and (2) implementing a self-correction strategy at test time.

**Strengths:**

*   **Significant Dataset Creation:** The creation of a 3.5 billion token reasoning dataset is a major contribution. This addresses the data scarcity problem, a recognized hurdle in RTL-specific LLM development. The use of detailed chain-of-thought explanations is well-motivated.
*   **Effective Test-Time Scaling:** The iterative self-correction mechanism is a clever way to extend reasoning at inference time. The choice of a corrective prompt based on general RTL coding guidelines is also reasonable.  The results show this does improve performance.
*   **State-of-the-Art Results:** The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating substantial improvements over existing baselines on established RTL benchmarks.  The comparison to other models trained on larger datasets is especially impactful.
*   **Careful Ablation Studies:** The authors perform ablation studies to understand the importance of both the reasoning data and the test-time scaling component. The analysis of the relationship between reasoning length and accuracy is insightful.
*   **Generalization Analysis:** The paper attempts to addresses concerns of overfitting by evaluating ScaleRTL on general-purpose coding benchmarks, demonstrating that the RTL-specific training does not significantly degrade its performance on broader tasks.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the methodology, including data collection, model training, and inference procedures, facilitating reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on DeepSeek-R1:** The approach relies heavily on DeepSeek-R1 for generating reasoning traces.  While DeepSeek-R1 is a state-of-the-art model, the quality of the generated traces directly impacts the effectiveness of ScaleRTL. The reasoning capability of the final model is inherently capped by the reasoning capability of DeepSeek-R1. A more in-depth analysis of the errors made by DeepSeek-R1 is worthwhile.
*   **Reasoning Rule Generalization:** The general RTL coding rules used in the corrective prompt, while helpful, may lack specificity. It is possible that more targeted error analysis and creation of rule based prompts would allow for more performance improvements.
*   **Limited Generalization:**  While tested on general coding benchmarks, the improvement on these other datasets is not remarkable. This raises questions about whether the gains on RTL benchmarks are truly due to enhanced *reasoning* or simply specialized memorization. More complex general coding tasks should be tested.
*  **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:** The paper could benefit from a qualitative analysis of the generated code and reasoning traces, showcasing the types of errors that ScaleRTL successfully corrects. While some examples are provided, more detail would provide more insight.
*   **Limited Agentic Interaction:** The paper compares the approach against agentic approaches but dismisses them due to tool integration needs. While the work focuses on intrinsic model capabilities, it would be compelling to explore integration of the model as the basis of a broader agent for RTL generation to understand if performance can be increased further.

**Justification of Score:**

ScaleRTL represents a significant step forward in applying LLMs to RTL code generation. The creation of the large-scale reasoning dataset and the introduction of the iterative self-correction mechanism are novel and effective. The experimental results are convincing and the analysis is thorough. The reliance on DeepSeek-R1 and the limited demonstration of reasoning on general coding tasks are weaknesses, however, they do not detract from the overall contribution. The paper addresses a very important problem in hardware design and contributes a compelling solution. It would certainly have broad impact within the field.

### Ravan: Multi-Head Low-Rank Adaptation for Federated Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05568v1)
- **Authors**: Arian Raje, Baris Askin, Divyansh Jhunjhunwala, Gauri Joshi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have not yet effectively leveraged the vast amounts of edge-device data, and federated learning (FL) offers a promising paradigm to collaboratively fine-tune LLMs without transferring private edge data to the cloud. To operate within the computation and communication constraints of edge devices, recent literature on federated fine-tuning of LLMs proposes the use of low-rank adaptation (LoRA) and similar parameter-efficient methods. However, LoRA-based methods suffer from accuracy degradation in FL settings, primarily because of data and computational heterogeneity across clients. We propose \textsc{Ravan}, an adaptive multi-head LoRA method that balances parameter efficiency and model expressivity by reparameterizing the weight updates as the sum of multiple LoRA heads $s_i\textbf{B}_i\textbf{H}_i\textbf{A}_i$ in which only the core matrices $\textbf{H}_i$ and their lightweight scaling factors $s_i$ are trained. These trainable scaling factors let the optimization focus on the most useful heads, recovering a higher-rank approximation of the full update without increasing the number of communicated parameters since clients upload $s_i\textbf{H}_i$ directly. Experiments on vision and language benchmarks show that \textsc{Ravan} improves test accuracy by 2-8\% over prior parameter-efficient baselines, making it a robust and scalable solution for federated fine-tuning of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces RAVAN, a new federated learning (FL) method for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on edge devices. RAVAN tackles the challenges of data and computational heterogeneity in FL by using an adaptive multi-head low-rank adaptation (LoRA) approach. The key idea is to re-parameterize weight updates as a weighted sum of multiple LoRA heads, where only scaling factors and core matrices are trained. This allows for a balance between parameter efficiency and model expressiveness. The authors conduct experiments on vision and language benchmarks, demonstrating that RAVAN improves test accuracy compared to existing parameter-efficient FL methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to federated fine-tuning of LLMs by introducing the concept of adaptive multi-head LoRA. While LoRA and multi-head architectures are not new individually, their combination and adaptation for the specific challenges of FL (data heterogeneity, computational constraints, and exact aggregation) represent a significant contribution. The idea of selectively training heads based on client resources and the design to ensure exact aggregation are also novel and important.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its ability to enable more effective federated fine-tuning of LLMs on resource-constrained edge devices. FL is a promising paradigm for leveraging edge data while preserving privacy, and the RAVAN method helps to overcome the limitations of existing parameter-efficient methods in heterogeneous FL settings. The improved accuracy and robustness demonstrated in the experiments suggest that RAVAN can contribute to the widespread adoption of LLMs in edge applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of data and computational heterogeneity in federated LLM fine-tuning.
    *   **Technically sound:** The proposed method is based on solid theoretical foundations (LoRA, multi-head architectures, and exact aggregation).
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The authors conduct experiments on various vision and language benchmarks with different settings (IID/non-IID, varying clients, varying compute).
    *   **Strong results:** The experimental results consistently demonstrate that RAVAN outperforms existing parameter-efficient FL methods.
    *   **Thorough ablation studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of different components of the RAVAN method (initialization, head selection strategies, scaling factors).
    *   **Scalability demonstrated:** The LLaMA3.2-1B experiments highlight the potential of RAVAN for scaling to larger model architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The number of heads to select is manually defined and not adaptive to the data. Even though an algorithm has been proposed to select relevant heads based on the importance of the data, this may not be enough.
    *   The experiments, while comprehensive, are limited to specific benchmarks and datasets. It would be beneficial to evaluate RAVAN on more diverse and realistic FL scenarios.
    *   The paper does not explore the potential impact of differential privacy (DP) on the performance of RAVAN.
    *   The authors did not include a runtime analysis comparing the efficiency of RAVAN and the baselines.
    *   Although the authors discuss different head selection strategies, they are still limited to selecting the same number of heads in each layer.
    *   The current implementation does not consider personalized federated learning.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   RAVAN can be a building block for developing more advanced and practical federated LLM fine-tuning methods.
    *   The method may inspire future research on adaptive and resource-aware FL algorithms.
    *   The experimental results and insights can inform the design of LLM-based edge applications.

*   **Justification for the Score:**

The paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the challenging problem of federated LLM fine-tuning. The proposed method is well-motivated, technically sound, and supported by comprehensive experimental results. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. It's a practical solution with demonstrated improvements, and has a strong potential to influence future research and development in this area. However, there are potential improvements, as aforementioned.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### PartCrafter: Structured 3D Mesh Generation via Compositional Latent Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05573v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Lin, Chenguo Lin, Panwang Pan, Honglei Yan, Yiqiang Feng, Yadong Mu, Katerina Fragkiadaki
- **Abstract**: We introduce PartCrafter, the first structured 3D generative model that jointly synthesizes multiple semantically meaningful and geometrically distinct 3D meshes from a single RGB image. Unlike existing methods that either produce monolithic 3D shapes or follow two-stage pipelines, i.e., first segmenting an image and then reconstructing each segment, PartCrafter adopts a unified, compositional generation architecture that does not rely on pre-segmented inputs. Conditioned on a single image, it simultaneously denoises multiple 3D parts, enabling end-to-end part-aware generation of both individual objects and complex multi-object scenes. PartCrafter builds upon a pretrained 3D mesh diffusion transformer (DiT) trained on whole objects, inheriting the pretrained weights, encoder, and decoder, and introduces two key innovations: (1) A compositional latent space, where each 3D part is represented by a set of disentangled latent tokens; (2) A hierarchical attention mechanism that enables structured information flow both within individual parts and across all parts, ensuring global coherence while preserving part-level detail during generation. To support part-level supervision, we curate a new dataset by mining part-level annotations from large-scale 3D object datasets. Experiments show that PartCrafter outperforms existing approaches in generating decomposable 3D meshes, including parts that are not directly visible in input images, demonstrating the strength of part-aware generative priors for 3D understanding and synthesis. Code and training data will be released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PartCrafter paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PartCrafter, a novel structured 3D generative model that generates multiple semantically meaningful and geometrically distinct 3D meshes from a single RGB image. Unlike previous methods that produce monolithic 3D shapes or require a two-stage segmentation and reconstruction pipeline, PartCrafter adopts a unified, compositional generation architecture, eliminating the need for pre-segmented inputs.  The model builds upon a pretrained 3D mesh diffusion transformer (DiT) and introduces two key innovations: (1) a compositional latent space where each 3D part is represented by disentangled latent tokens, and (2) a hierarchical attention mechanism that enables structured information flow both within and across parts.  The authors also curate a new dataset with part-level annotations to support part-level supervision.  Experiments demonstrate that PartCrafter outperforms existing approaches in generating decomposable 3D meshes, even inferring parts not directly visible in the input image.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty in several aspects:
    *   **Unified Compositional Generation:** The most significant contribution is the shift away from two-stage segmentation-then-reconstruction pipelines towards a unified, end-to-end compositional generative architecture. This is a genuinely new approach in 3D generation.
    *   **Compositional Latent Space:** The idea of using disentangled latent tokens to represent individual 3D parts within a larger scene is innovative. This allows for independent editing and manipulation of parts, something lacking in many previous methods.
    *   **Hierarchical Attention:** The local-global attention mechanism enables a structured information flow, allowing PartCrafter to maintain global coherence while preserving part-level detail. This is a well-designed architectural component.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Improved Generation Quality:** Experimental results show superior generation quality and efficiency compared to existing methods, particularly in generating invisible parts. This is a tangible improvement over the state-of-the-art.
    *   **Dataset Curation:** Curating a new dataset with part annotations from existing 3D object repositories is a valuable contribution to the community and enables training of such models.
    *   **Universal Model:**  The ability to generate at both object and scene level is significant, suggesting it could be the start of a more general-purpose generation model.
    *   **Impact on Downstream Tasks:** The ability to decompose objects into parts has significant implications for downstream tasks such as texture mapping, animation, physical simulation, and scene editing.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Technical Soundness:** The paper presents a clear and well-defined methodology, supported by thorough experiments and ablation studies. The architectural design choices are justified, and the results are convincing.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation metrics are appropriate for the task, and the comparison against baselines is fair and well-controlled.  The use of ablation studies helps to isolate the contributions of different components.
    *   **Good Writing and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, with clear figures and tables.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** While the dataset is a valuable contribution, the paper acknowledges that it's relatively small compared to datasets used for monolithic 3D object generation. Scaling up training with larger datasets could potentially lead to even better results.
    *   **Reliance on Pretrained Model:**  The method builds upon a pretrained TripoSG model, meaning its performance is limited by the capabilities of the base model.
    *   **Qualitative imperfections**: The qualitative results show some inaccuracies in part generation.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   PartCrafter has the potential to significantly impact the field of 3D generation by providing a more flexible and controllable way to create 3D assets.
    *   The compositional latent space and hierarchical attention mechanism could inspire new architectures for other generative models.
    *   The curated dataset could become a valuable resource for training other part-aware 3D models.

**Score:** 8.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel and significant contribution to the field of 3D generation. The unified compositional generation architecture, compositional latent space, and hierarchical attention mechanism are all innovative and well-designed. Experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of PartCrafter, and the curated dataset is a valuable resource. The model shows promise and addresses important limitations in current 3D generation techniques. The weaknesses – namely, the relatively small dataset size and reliance on a pretrained model – are acknowledged by the authors and represent opportunities for future work, rather than fundamental flaws in the approach. The qualitative results, while generally very good, show room for improvement as well, which is reflected in the score. The paper offers a significant advancement over previous approaches and is likely to influence future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### When can in-context learning generalize out of task distribution?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05574v1)
- **Authors**: Chase Goddard, Lindsay M. Smith, Vudtiwat Ngampruetikorn, David J. Schwab
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) is a remarkable capability of pretrained transformers that allows models to generalize to unseen tasks after seeing only a few examples. We investigate empirically the conditions necessary on the pretraining distribution for ICL to emerge and generalize \emph{out-of-distribution}. Previous work has focused on the number of distinct tasks necessary in the pretraining dataset. Here, we use a different notion of task diversity to study the emergence of ICL in transformers trained on linear functions. We find that as task diversity increases, transformers undergo a transition from a specialized solution, which exhibits ICL only within the pretraining task distribution, to a solution which generalizes out of distribution to the entire task space. We also investigate the nature of the solutions learned by the transformer on both sides of the transition, and observe similar transitions in nonlinear regression problems. We construct a phase diagram to characterize how our concept of task diversity interacts with the number of pretraining tasks. In addition, we explore how factors such as the depth of the model and the dimensionality of the regression problem influence the transition.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "When can in-context learning generalize out of task distribution?"

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the conditions under which in-context learning (ICL) in transformers can generalize to tasks outside the pretraining distribution. Unlike prior work focusing on the sheer number of pretraining tasks, this study examines *task diversity* in terms of similarity between tasks, particularly in the context of learning linear functions. The authors find that as task diversity increases (i.e., the tasks in the pretraining set become more dissimilar), transformers undergo a transition from a "specialized" ICL solution, effective only within the pretraining distribution, to a more "general" solution capable of out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization across the entire task space. They also explore the nature of these solutions and construct a phase diagram characterizing the interplay between task diversity and the number of pretraining tasks. Finally, they observe similar transitions in non-linear regression problems and study how the depth of the transformer and dimensionality of the input impact the specialization-generalization transition.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's key novelty lies in its shift in focus from merely *counting* pretraining tasks to examining the *nature* of the tasks themselves and the degree to which they are diverse.  The observation of a specialization-generalization *phase transition* as a function of task diversity is a significant contribution. Prior work, especially Raventos et al. (2023), established the importance of the number of pretraining tasks. This paper builds upon that by showing that how different these tasks are plays a critical role in ICL generalization. Examining out-of-distribution generalization in *tasks*, not samples, adds a valuable new perspective.

*   **Significance:** Understanding the conditions for OOD generalization in ICL is crucial for building reliable and trustworthy language models. This work offers insights into how to design pretraining datasets to encourage ICL that extends beyond the seen tasks. The comparison to Bayes-optimal solutions and the analysis of different generalization regimes (IWL, in-task generalization, and out-of-task generalization) add to the significance. The observation that depth and dimension doesn't strongly affect the transition point suggests that the nature of pretraining data is crucial than other aspects.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly frames the problem of OOD generalization for ICL tasks.
    *   **Controlled Experiments:** The use of linear and controlled non-linear functions allows for rigorous experimentation and analysis.
    *   **Well-defined Metrics:**  The use of MSE, NSR and comparing the transformer's performance to Bayesian and OLS solutions provides a solid basis for quantitative evaluation.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper explores the interplay between multiple factors: task diversity, number of tasks, dimensionality, and model depth.
    *   **Phase Diagram:**  The phase diagram effectively summarizes the different regimes of ICL generalization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:**  The focus on linear and controlled non-linear regression problems, while enabling rigorous analysis, limits the immediate applicability of the results to real-world, high-dimensional language tasks.  It remains to be seen how the observed transitions manifest in more complex settings.
    *   **GPT-2 Style Architecture:** The architecture used in the work is older architecture, meaning it lacks some of the architectural features of models that have excellent in-context learning. Thus, it is unclear if the same principles apply to state-of-the-art architectures.
    *   **Lack of theoretical analysis:** While empirically well-done, the paper lacks a theoretical justification for the specialization/generalization transition. The results are more observational than explanatory.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of pretraining strategies for ICL. The emphasis on task diversity as a key factor can guide the creation of more effective pretraining datasets.  It will likely spur further research into the theoretical underpinnings of these transitions and the development of new metrics for quantifying task similarity.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

**Rationale:** The paper provides a novel perspective on ICL generalization by focusing on task diversity rather than simply the number of pretraining tasks.  The empirical findings are solid, the analysis is comprehensive, and the phase diagram provides a useful framework. However, the limited scope of the experiments (linear and simple non-linear functions) and the lack of theoretical explanation weaken the paper. A more convincing set of experiments would extend to richer models and more complex data. While the focus is on a simplified setting to enable controlled experiments, the results should be interpreted with caution when considering more complex, real-world ICL tasks. Although the approach is innovative, the results require further investigation into real-world scenarios to show that pretraining datasets need to be carefully designed to encourage ICL that extends beyond the seen tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
