### Conformal Prediction Adaptive to Unknown Subpopulation Shifts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05583v1)
- **Authors**: Nien-Shao Wang, Duygu Nur Yaldiz, Yavuz Faruk Bakman, Sai Praneeth Karimireddy
- **Abstract**: Conformal prediction is widely used to equip black-box machine learning models with uncertainty quantification enjoying formal coverage guarantees. However, these guarantees typically break down in the presence of distribution shifts, where the data distribution at test time differs from the training (or calibration-time) distribution. In this work, we address subpopulation shifts, where the test environment exhibits an unknown and differing mixture of subpopulations compared to the calibration data. We propose new methods that provably adapt conformal prediction to such shifts, ensuring valid coverage without requiring explicit knowledge of subpopulation structure. Our algorithms scale to high-dimensional settings and perform effectively in realistic machine learning tasks. Extensive experiments on vision (with vision transformers) and language (with large language models) benchmarks demonstrate that our methods reliably maintain coverage and controls risk in scenarios where standard conformal prediction fails.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of conformal prediction (CP) under subpopulation shifts, where the distribution of data at test time differs from the calibration data due to variations in the mixture of subpopulations. Standard conformal prediction guarantees break down in such scenarios. The authors propose new methods to adapt CP to these shifts, ensuring valid coverage without requiring explicit knowledge of the subpopulation structure. These methods involve using a learned domain classifier to estimate the probability of a test input belonging to different subpopulations and then weighing the calibration data accordingly. The paper presents algorithms with theoretical coverage guarantees, scalable to high-dimensional settings. Experiments on vision and language benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods in maintaining coverage and controlling risk compared to standard CP.  The paper also considers a scenario where domain labels are unavailable and propose a similarity-based weighting scheme for calibration data. They extend their methods to risk control for hallucination detection in large language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its explicit focus on adapting conformal prediction to *unknown* subpopulation shifts.  Prior works address distribution shifts in general, or require knowledge of the shift parameters (e.g., knowing the source and target distributions, or having access to data from the target distribution). The paper proposes methods that are adaptive and do not require such explicit knowledge, learning the subpopulation structure indirectly via a domain classifier. The use of multicalibration or multiaccuracy assumptions on the domain classifier to provide theoretical guarantees, while common in fairness literature, seems relatively novel in the conformal prediction domain. The similarity based adaptation in the absence of domain labels is also a good direction.

* **Significance:** Conformal prediction is a crucial technique for uncertainty quantification in machine learning, particularly in high-stakes applications. The breakdown of CP guarantees under distribution shifts limits its real-world applicability. By addressing the specific and common problem of subpopulation shifts *without requiring explicit knowledge of shifts*, the paper makes a significant step towards robust and reliable uncertainty quantification in dynamic and diverse environments.  The experiments on realistic vision and language tasks (including LLM hallucination detection) strengthen the practical significance of the work.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly defines the problem of conformal prediction under unknown subpopulation shifts.
    * **Theoretically grounded:** The proposed algorithms come with theoretical guarantees for marginal coverage under mild assumptions (Bayes optimality, multicalibration, or multiaccuracy of the domain classifier).
    * **Practical applicability:**  The methods scale to high-dimensional settings and are effective in real-world machine learning tasks.  The LLM hallucination detection application is particularly relevant.
    * **Empirical validation:** Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks (vision and language) convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    * **Exploration of various scenarios**: The paper explores different variants and assumptions (with/without domain information, etc.), providing a comprehensive analysis.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Domain Classifier**: The performance of the proposed method hinges on the quality of the learned domain classifier. While the paper considers multicalibration and multiaccuracy to address potential classifier inaccuracies, the domain classifier could still be a bottleneck.  The paper doesn't delve deeply into the challenges of training such classifiers in practice, specifically in high-dimensional settings, or analyze the robustness of the approach to poorly trained domain classifiers.
    * **Independence Assumption in Table 2**: The paper acknowledges that the assumptions are strong in Table 2 which limits the gains compared to standard approaches.
    * **Limited Novelty in Risk Control extension:** While adapting to hallucination detection is valuable, the conformal risk control framework itself is not novel to this work.
    * **Choice of score function**: The paper provides limited guidance on what scoring functions would work.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of conformal prediction, particularly in areas where distribution shifts are common (e.g., healthcare, finance, autonomous driving). The work provides a practical and theoretically sound approach to adapting CP to these shifts, making uncertainty quantification more robust and reliable. It opens up several avenues for future research, such as developing more robust domain classifiers or extending the methods to other types of distribution shifts.

* **Justification for Score:** The paper offers a valuable contribution with practical implications, theoretical grounding, and strong empirical validation.  The problem addressed is significant, and the proposed methods are demonstrably effective. The biggest weakness is the reliance on the domain classifier, which could be improved with more in-depth analysis and strategies for robust training. Considering the other strengths, I'm assigning a score that reflects the significant contribution, but acknowledging the room for further refinement,
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### TabFlex: Scaling Tabular Learning to Millions with Linear Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05584v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Zeng, Tuan Dinh, Wonjun Kang, Andreas C Mueller
- **Abstract**: Leveraging the in-context learning (ICL) capability of Large Language Models (LLMs) for tabular classification has gained significant attention for its training-free adaptability across diverse datasets. Recent advancements, like TabPFN, excel in small-scale tabular datasets but struggle to scale for large and complex datasets. Our work enhances the efficiency and scalability of TabPFN for larger datasets by incorporating linear attention mechanisms as a scalable alternative to complexity-quadratic self-attention. Our model, TabFlex, efficiently handles tabular datasets with thousands of features and hundreds of classes, scaling seamlessly to millions of samples. For instance, TabFlex processes the poker-hand dataset with over a million samples in just 5 seconds. Our extensive evaluations demonstrate that TabFlex can achieve over a 2x speedup compared to TabPFN and a 1.5x speedup over XGBoost, outperforming 25 tested baselines in terms of efficiency across a diverse range of datasets. Furthermore, TabFlex remains highly effective on large-scale datasets, delivering strong performance with significantly reduced computational costs, especially when combined with data-efficient techniques such as dimensionality reduction and data sampling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TABFLEX: Scaling Tabular Learning to Millions with Linear Attention":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the scalability limitations of TABPFN, a Transformer-based model that leverages in-context learning (ICL) for tabular classification. TABPFN excels on small tabular datasets but struggles with larger, more complex ones due to the quadratic complexity of its self-attention mechanism. The authors propose TABFLEX, which replaces the softmax attention in TABPFN with linear attention.  They systematically evaluate state-space models and linear attention as potential replacements and conclude that linear attention provides the best balance between performance and efficiency in the ICL tabular setting. TABFLEX is implemented as a suite of models adapted to different dataset characteristics and can efficiently process tabular datasets with thousands of features, hundreds of classes, and millions of samples. The authors demonstrate TABFLEX's superior performance and computational efficiency compared to TABPFN and other baselines (including XGBoost) across various datasets and conducts ablation studies to further improve efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of replacing softmax attention with linear attention to improve the scalability of TABPFN is incremental but well-motivated and empirically sound. Applying linear attention to an ICL-based tabular learning framework is a logical step, and the paper demonstrates its effectiveness. The authors also explore SSMs, but the finding that causal attention performs worse than non-causal attention, therefore not a suitable choice for SSMs, is non-novel and has been previously demonstrated (as the authors correctly highlight). The three model types adapting TABFLEX to different dataset dimensions are a pragmatic engineering contribution, but not deeply novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in using Transformer-based models for tabular data: scalability. TABFLEX's ability to handle much larger datasets than TABPFN opens up possibilities for applying ICL to real-world problems involving millions of samples. The gains in computational efficiency, especially the speedup compared to XGBoost on very large datasets, are valuable. While ICL for tabular data is still an emerging area, the work contributes to making it a more practical alternative to traditional methods. The ablation studies, particularly those examining the combination of TABFLEX with dimensionality reduction and data sampling techniques, provide valuable insights into further optimizing its performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation across a diverse range of datasets.
    *   Detailed ablation studies exploring different architectural choices and data-efficient techniques.
    *   Clear presentation of the method and results.
    *   Addresses a relevant and practical problem in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The core idea is incremental, building directly upon TABPFN and existing linear attention mechanisms.
    *   The model selection strategy feels somewhat ad-hoc and may not generalize well to unseen dataset distributions.
    *   Limited exploration of alternative architectures beyond linear attention. The authors do not explore newer variants of linear attention that improve the expressivity of the attention mechanism.
    *   While results on regression using a discretized target are shown, the approach seems somewhat naive and limits the applicability of TABFLEX.
    *   The adaptation to image classification, although interesting, seems rudimentary and requires further investigation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on ICL for tabular data. Other researchers can build upon TABFLEX to develop even more scalable and efficient tabular learning models, perhaps by exploring other efficient attention mechanisms or by incorporating more sophisticated data-efficient techniques. The insights regarding the importance of non-causal attention for tabular ICL are also valuable.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a solid engineering contribution by adapting linear attention to an existing, promising framework (TABPFN). It demonstrably improves the scalability of ICL for tabular data, making it more practical for real-world applications. The thorough experimental evaluation provides valuable insights. However, the core idea is incremental, and the lack of deeper architectural innovation or theoretical grounding prevents it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### UTSA-NLP at ArchEHR-QA 2025: Improving EHR Question Answering via Self-Consistency Prompting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05589v1)
- **Authors**: Sara Shields-Menard, Zach Reimers, Joshua Gardner, David Perry, Anthony Rios
- **Abstract**: We describe our system for the ArchEHR-QA Shared Task on answering clinical questions using electronic health records (EHRs). Our approach uses large language models in two steps: first, to find sentences in the EHR relevant to a clinician's question, and second, to generate a short, citation-supported response based on those sentences. We use few-shot prompting, self-consistency, and thresholding to improve the sentence classification step to decide which sentences are essential. We compare several models and find that a smaller 8B model performs better than a larger 70B model for identifying relevant information. Our results show that accurate sentence selection is critical for generating high-quality responses and that self-consistency with thresholding helps make these decisions more reliable.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes a system developed for the ArchEHR-QA shared task, which focuses on answering clinical questions using electronic health records (EHRs). The system employs a two-step approach: first, identifying relevant sentences within the EHR based on the clinician's question, and second, generating a concise, citation-supported answer from those selected sentences. The authors use a combination of few-shot prompting, self-consistency, and thresholding to improve the sentence classification process. They found that a smaller 8B parameter LLaMA model outperformed a larger 70B model in identifying relevant information. The results emphasize the importance of accurate sentence selection for generating high-quality responses, and demonstrate that self-consistency with thresholding improves the reliability of these decisions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Problem:** The paper addresses a significant and pressing problem in healthcare: clinician burnout due to the increasing volume of patient-initiated messages and results.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The two-step approach (sentence selection and answer generation) provides a clear and modular framework.
    *   **Effective Techniques:** The use of few-shot prompting, self-consistency, and thresholding are well-established techniques in NLP, and the paper demonstrates their effective application in the EHR context.
    *   **Surprising Result:** The finding that a smaller model outperformed a larger model in the sentence selection task is notable and worth investigating further. This suggests that certain model architectures or training regimes may be better suited for specific tasks in the medical domain.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablations on self-consistency and thresholding are valuable as they highlight the contribution of these techniques to the overall system performance.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis provides valuable insights into the types of errors made by the model, such as failing to recognize contextual relationships and relying too heavily on lexical overlap. This helps guide future research directions.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset:** The use of a relatively small dataset (20 development cases and 100 test cases) limits the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:**  Reliance solely on quantitative metrics without human review of patient context and medical accuracy is a significant limitation. The lack of evaluation of personable aspects (empathy, professionalism) is also a drawback.
    *   **Heuristic Thresholding:** The reliance on a heuristic approach to thresholding could be improved.
    *   **Independent Sentence Classification:** Treating each sentence independently during classification may miss critical contextual information.
    *   **Overly Specific Setup:** The paper focuses on a limited setup in relation to response length restrictions.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of techniques (few-shot prompting, self-consistency, thresholding) applied to the task of question answering using EHR data. The finding that the smaller model performed better in information retrieval is also noteworthy and might indicate a new direction for the community to research. The problem itself is very significant. The significance lies in addressing clinician burden and potentially improving patient care by providing clinicians with efficient access to relevant information within EHRs. The error analysis is valuable in guiding future research.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    The paper has the potential to influence future research by highlighting the importance of accurate sentence selection in EHR question answering and by demonstrating the effectiveness of self-consistency and thresholding techniques. The finding regarding the smaller model might prompt further investigation into the optimal model size and architecture for specific tasks in the medical domain. The error analysis also provides valuable directions for future work.

*   **Improvements:**

    Future work should focus on using larger and more diverse datasets, incorporating human evaluation of patient context and medical accuracy, developing adaptive thresholding methods, and exploring document-level context and sequential modeling. Fine-tuning the models with clinician feedback would also be highly valuable.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper addresses a critical issue, presents a clear and well-executed approach, and provides valuable insights through ablation studies and error analysis. The finding regarding the smaller model is interesting and could have implications for future research. However, the limitations associated with the small dataset, the reliance on quantitative metrics, and the heuristic thresholding approach significantly impact the overall score. While the application of established NLP techniques to this specific EHR task is valuable, it's not a groundbreaking innovation. Thus, while it makes a solid contribution, it falls short of being exceptionally novel or impactful.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Neural Diarization through Speaker Attribute Attractors and Local Dependency Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05593v1)
- **Authors**: David Palzer, Matthew Maciejewski, Eric Fosler-Lussier
- **Abstract**: In recent years, end-to-end approaches have made notable progress in addressing the challenge of speaker diarization, which involves segmenting and identifying speakers in multi-talker recordings. One such approach, Encoder-Decoder Attractors (EDA), has been proposed to handle variable speaker counts as well as better guide the network during training. In this study, we extend the attractor paradigm by moving beyond direct speaker modeling and instead focus on representing more detailed `speaker attributes' through a multi-stage process of intermediate representations. Additionally, we enhance the architecture by replacing transformers with conformers, a convolution-augmented transformer, to model local dependencies. Experiments demonstrate improved diarization performance on the CALLHOME dataset.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces improvements to neural speaker diarization, specifically focusing on the Encoder-Decoder Attractor (EDA) model. The key contributions are:

1.  **Speaker Attribute Attractors:** Moving beyond directly modeling speakers, the paper proposes representing more detailed 'speaker attributes' through a multi-stage process. This aims to provide a more robust way to condition deeper layers of the model. This relaxes the requirement that each layer individually solve the diarization problem.
2.  **Local Dependency Modeling with Conformers:** Replacing transformers with conformers (convolution-augmented transformers) to better model local temporal dependencies in the audio data.
3.  **Ablation Studies:** The paper presents ablation studies to analyze the contribution of each component.

The experiments demonstrate improved diarization performance on the CALLHOME dataset.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The individual components of the proposed approach (Attribute Attractors and Conformer integration) are not entirely novel. Intermediate attractors are used in [20]. Conformers have already been used in tasks like ASR [22]. The novelty lies in their combination within the EDA architecture for diarization.

*   **Significance:** The improvements shown on the CALLHOME dataset suggest that the proposed approach is promising. By breaking down the speaker modeling into speaker attributes and then combining them, it improves speaker differentiation in noisy conditions. Additionally, the ablation study provides insights into the effectiveness of different components, further strengthening the contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a crucial limitation of the original EEND and EDA models: effectively handling variable numbers of speakers and improving diarization accuracy.
    *   The introduction of speaker attribute attractors is a reasonably well motivated method and provides a mechanism for modeling more detailed speaker characteristics.
    *   The use of conformers demonstrably improves performance by leveraging local temporal context.
    *   The Ablation studies show some interesting findings about what is working and what is not

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited to the CALLHOME dataset. While this is a common benchmark, evaluations on other datasets (e.g., more challenging meeting recordings or noisy environments) would provide a more robust assessment of the approach's generalizability.
    *   The paper lacks a detailed analysis of the learned speaker attributes. Further explanation of what constitutes these attributes would be very interesting and increase the impact of the work.
    *   The absolute gains in performance are incremental improvements, but not a paradigm shift. This is a gradual refinement of an existing architecture.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a useful step towards better diarization systems. The idea of disentangling speaker modeling into attribute modeling is promising and can inspire future research. The use of conformers in this context reinforces the value of local dependency modeling.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a non-trivial improvement to the EDA architecture for neural diarization. While it leverages existing concepts (conformers, attribute attractors), the specific combination and the focus on speaker attribute modeling represent a valuable contribution. The performance improvements are positive.

Given the incremental nature of the improvement and the limitations in the experimental evaluation, a score of **7** out of 10 is appropriate.
**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### SoK: Are Watermarks in LLMs Ready for Deployment?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05594v1)
- **Authors**: Kieu Dang, Phung Lai, NhatHai Phan, Yelong Shen, Ruoming Jin, Abdallah Khreishah, My Thai
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed natural language processing, demonstrating impressive capabilities across diverse tasks. However, deploying these models introduces critical risks related to intellectual property violations and potential misuse, particularly as adversaries can imitate these models to steal services or generate misleading outputs. We specifically focus on model stealing attacks, as they are highly relevant to proprietary LLMs and pose a serious threat to their security, revenue, and ethical deployment. While various watermarking techniques have emerged to mitigate these risks, it remains unclear how far the community and industry have progressed in developing and deploying watermarks in LLMs. To bridge this gap, we aim to develop a comprehensive systematization for watermarks in LLMs by 1) presenting a detailed taxonomy for watermarks in LLMs, 2) proposing a novel intellectual property classifier to explore the effectiveness and impacts of watermarks on LLMs under both attack and attack-free environments, 3) analyzing the limitations of existing watermarks in LLMs, and 4) discussing practical challenges and potential future directions for watermarks in LLMs. Through extensive experiments, we show that despite promising research outcomes and significant attention from leading companies and community to deploy watermarks, these techniques have yet to reach their full potential in real-world applications due to their unfavorable impacts on model utility of LLMs and downstream tasks. Our findings provide an insightful understanding of watermarks in LLMs, highlighting the need for practical watermarks solutions tailored to LLM deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematization of knowledge (SoK) study focused on the deployment readiness of watermarks (WMs) for Large Language Models (LLMs). It aims to comprehensively evaluate the effectiveness and practicality of WMs in mitigating model-stealing attacks. The study introduces a detailed taxonomy for WMs, proposes a novel cross-model IP classifier to assess WM impacts on both LLM utility and IP protection, analyzes the limitations of existing WMs, and discusses practical challenges and future directions. Through extensive experiments across various WMs, LLMs, and IP checkers, the paper concludes that while WMs enhance uniqueness and distinctiveness of LLM outputs, their impact on model utility and vulnerabilities to attacks prevent their immediate real-world deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by providing a comprehensive SoK on LLM watermarking. The taxonomy is well-structured, providing a useful overview of different WM techniques. The cross-model IP classifier is a novel approach to assessing WM effectiveness across different LLMs, which is more realistic than testing each LLM and WM in isolation.
*   **Significance:** Model stealing is a critical issue for proprietary LLMs, and the ability to effectively watermark and detect unauthorized use is essential for protecting IP. This paper identifies limitations of current approaches and provides insights into the trade-offs between IP protection, model utility, and robustness against attacks.  It highlights the need for more sophisticated WM solutions.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper covers a broad range of WMs, LLMs, and IP checkers, providing a thorough evaluation of the field.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper focuses on the practical challenges and limitations of deploying WMs in real-world applications, which is crucial for guiding future research.
    *   **Novel Methodology:** The cross-model IP classifier is a valuable tool for assessing WM effectiveness in realistic scenarios.
    *   **Thorough Experimentation:** The experiments are well-designed and conducted, providing strong evidence for the paper's conclusions.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Attack Scenarios:** While the paper considers attack scenarios (WM Removal, Dipper, Substitution), a deeper exploration of other attack vectors and defensive strategies would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Perplexity Observations:** While Perplexity is evaluated for WMs as a utility measurement, the relationship between that and attack robustness/preservation could be clearer.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Parameter Robustness:** The paper focuses on a limited number of publicly available LLM checkpoints (or pre-trained models). Expanding to include other popular models would bolster the validity of the observations and conclusions.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper will likely be influential in guiding future research on LLM watermarking. The taxonomy, experimental results, and identified challenges provide a solid foundation for developing more practical and effective WM solutions.
*   **Overclaims:** The paper claims a "novel IP classifier," but while it is a new combination of techniques, it leverages well-established machine learning methodologies. The real novelty resides in how it is deployed across multiple LLMs in the SoK effort.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive analysis of the current state of LLM watermarking. The identified limitations and challenges are crucial for guiding future research, and the proposed cross-model IP classifier is a valuable tool for assessing WM effectiveness. However, there is some lack of exploration of specific attacks, and minor details to be improved.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### FaCTR: Factorized Channel-Temporal Representation Transformers for Efficient Time Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05597v1)
- **Authors**: Yash Vijay, Harini Subramanyan
- **Abstract**: While Transformers excel in language and vision-where inputs are semantically rich and exhibit univariate dependency structures-their architectural complexity leads to diminishing returns in time series forecasting. Time series data is characterized by low per-timestep information density and complex dependencies across channels and covariates, requiring conditioning on structured variable interactions. To address this mismatch and overparameterization, we propose FaCTR, a lightweight spatiotemporal Transformer with an explicitly structural design. FaCTR injects dynamic, symmetric cross-channel interactions-modeled via a low-rank Factorization Machine into temporally contextualized patch embeddings through a learnable gating mechanism. It further encodes static and dynamic covariates for multivariate conditioning. Despite its compact design, FaCTR achieves state-of-the-art performance on eleven public forecasting benchmarks spanning both short-term and long-term horizons, with its largest variant using close to only 400K parameters-on average 50x smaller than competitive spatiotemporal transformer baselines. In addition, its structured design enables interpretability through cross-channel influence scores-an essential requirement for real-world decision-making. Finally, FaCTR supports self-supervised pretraining, positioning it as a compact yet versatile foundation for downstream time series tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the FaCTR paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FaCTR (Factorized Channel-Temporal Representation Transformer), a lightweight spatiotemporal Transformer architecture designed for efficient and interpretable multivariate time series forecasting.  FaCTR addresses the limitations of standard Transformers in this domain, which often suffer from overparameterization and a failure to explicitly model the generative structure of real-world time series data (specifically the complex dependencies between channels and covariates). FaCTR achieves this by:

1.  **Structural Disentanglement:** Decoupling temporal self-attention, cross-channel interaction (using a Factorization Machine), and embedding-wise mixing into distinct pathways.
2.  **Principled Cross-Channel Modeling:**  Employing a Factorization Machine (FM) to capture variable-wise dependencies across channels, treating inter-variable structure as an implicit interaction graph.
3.  **Compact and Scalable Design:**  Reducing parameters significantly compared to spatiotemporal Transformer baselines by avoiding dense spatiotemporal attention.
4.  **Interpretability:** Providing interpretable representations through temporal attention maps and FM scores.

The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on eleven public benchmarks, across various horizons, with significantly fewer parameters than existing models.  It also shows the model's ability to be pre-trained and fine-tuned, and its interpretability via a retail demand forecasting case study.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of Factorization Machines within the context of time series forecasting, particularly for capturing inter-channel dependencies. This is a relatively unexplored area, and the explicit structural design offers a compelling alternative to dense attention mechanisms or generic MLP-based mixers.  While FM's are well-established in recommendation, their adaptation and integration into a Transformer-based framework for time series is a significant contribution. The disentangled spatiotemporal design is also novel.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing the growing need for efficient and interpretable time series forecasting models.  The parameter efficiency is particularly valuable, making the model more practical for resource-constrained environments.  The interpretability aspect is also crucial for real-world applications, as it allows users to understand and trust the model's predictions. The results demonstrating that a far smaller model can outperform larger alternatives raises important questions about model design in the time series domain.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on a wide range of datasets and horizons, demonstrating consistent state-of-the-art performance.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches and motivates the need for a more efficient and interpretable solution.
    *   **Well-Designed Architecture:** The architecture is thoughtfully designed with a focus on structural priors and disentanglement, leading to improved performance and interpretability.
    *   **Interpretability Analysis:** The case study provides concrete examples of how the model's internal representations can be interpreted, enhancing trust and understanding.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Comparison to Graph-Based Methods:** While the paper benchmarks against some graph-based methods (CrossGNN), it could benefit from a more in-depth comparison to recent, more advanced graph neural networks tailored for time series, particularly those that incorporate covariates more effectively and have been showing improved performance.
    *   **Ablation Study Could Be More Granular:** The ablation study focuses on broad architectural components. More granular ablations, e.g., on the specific type of gating mechanism or the rank of the FM, could provide deeper insights into the importance of each design choice.
    *   **Synthetic Dataset Limitations:** While helpful for interpretability, the conclusions drawn from the synthetic retail dataset might not fully translate to more complex real-world scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** FaCTR has the potential to influence the design of future time series forecasting models, encouraging a shift towards more structured and efficient architectures. The use of FMs for capturing inter-channel dependencies could become a common practice.  The emphasis on interpretability is also a positive step towards building more trustworthy AI systems.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel architecture, FaCTR, that addresses key limitations in existing time series forecasting methods. The strong empirical results, coupled with the emphasis on interpretability and efficiency, make it a valuable contribution to the field. While there are some areas for improvement, the paper demonstrates a clear advancement over existing approaches and has the potential to influence future research.  I'm choosing to give it a score of 7. It is well above average, providing a distinct contribution to the field, but lacks a certain element of theoretical depth or breadth that would justify an exceptionally high score. The synthetic dataset while a useful tool, still creates some uncertainty as to just how well the model generalizes to more difficult real world datasets and as such, some of the claims are less definitive. The significant emphasis on interpretability earns a lot of marks, but more real world datasets could push this higher.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SynthesizeMe! Inducing Persona-Guided Prompts for Personalized Reward Models in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05598v1)
- **Authors**: Michael J Ryan, Omar Shaikh, Aditri Bhagirath, Daniel Frees, William Held, Diyi Yang
- **Abstract**: Recent calls for pluralistic alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) encourage adapting models to diverse user preferences. However, most prior work on personalized reward models heavily rely on additional identity information, such as demographic details or a predefined set of preference categories. To this end, we introduce SynthesizeMe, an approach to inducing synthetic user personas from user interactions for personalized reward modeling. SynthesizeMe first generates and verifies reasoning to explain user preferences, then induces synthetic user personas from that reasoning, and finally filters to informative prior user interactions in order to build personalized prompts for a particular user. We show that using SynthesizeMe induced prompts improves personalized LLM-as-a-judge accuracy by 4.4% on Chatbot Arena. Combining SynthesizeMe derived prompts with a reward model achieves top performance on PersonalRewardBench: a new curation of user-stratified interactions with chatbots collected from 854 users of Chatbot Arena and PRISM.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SynthesizeMe! Inducing Persona-Guided Prompts for Personalized Reward Models in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of personalizing reward models in large language models (LLMs) to align with diverse user preferences.  Instead of relying on explicit demographic information or predefined preference categories, the authors introduce SynthesizeMe, a method that induces synthetic user personas directly from user interactions. SynthesizeMe involves: 1) generating and verifying reasoning behind user preferences; 2) inducing synthetic user personas from the validated reasoning; and 3) filtering informative user interactions to create personalized prompts. The authors demonstrate that SynthesizeMe improves personalized LLM-as-a-judge accuracy and achieves top performance on a newly curated benchmark, PersonalRewardBench.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a novel approach to personalized reward modeling by leveraging LLMs to generate synthetic user personas from interaction data.  The idea of inducing personas from behavioral data (pairwise preferences) rather than relying on predefined categories or explicit user profiles is a significant and clever advance. SynthesizeMe offers a more flexible and data-driven approach compared to prior methods which typically require more information. Furthermore, the work makes good use of both reasoning and knowledge of LLMs which leads to creating personas.
*   **Significance:**  Personalized alignment is a crucial direction for LLM research. SynthesizeMe tackles a significant bottleneck in personalized reward modeling: data scarcity and the lack of explicit user preference information. By enabling effective personalization even with limited interaction data, the method paves the way for more practical and user-centric LLM applications. The performance improvements demonstrated on Chatbot Arena and the PersonalRewardBench benchmark highlight the effectiveness of the approach. The development of PersonalRewardBench is also a significant contribution that will allow other researchers to test their reward models. The fact that the personas are human readable and transferrable to API-only models enhances the significance of the study.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data-Driven Approach:** SynthesizeMe learns user preferences directly from interaction data, reducing the reliance on explicit user information or predefined categories.
    *   **Improved Performance:**  The method demonstrates significant improvements in personalized LLM-as-a-judge accuracy and achieves state-of-the-art performance on PersonalRewardBench.
    *   **Interpretability:**  SynthesizeMe produces natural language prompts that are interpretable and transferable between models.
    *   **Flexibility:** The technique is effective and is transferable to other methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scalability Analysis:** The scalability of SynthesizeMe with a very large user base, say millions of users, needs further exploration, especially the computation cost involved to generate and update prompts.
    *   **Sensitivity Analysis:** The paper needs to investigate how much the persona reflects the data used for analysis, how much it changes when a new prompt is introduced, and what bias is introduced or changed when introducing the analysis.
    *   **Lack of ablation for knowledge verification:** The paper did a study on different ablations of the SynthesizeMe pipeline but knowledge was never removed as a section. Further work needs to be done to verify the knowledge LLMs bring to the personas.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of personalized LLMs by providing a practical and effective method for inducing user personas from limited interaction data. The PersonalRewardBench benchmark will serve as a valuable resource for future research.  The approach of synthesizing personas to steer LLMs could be extended to other applications, such as personalized content generation or recommendation systems.

*   **Rationale for Score:** This paper tackles a significant problem in personalized LLMs, offers a novel and effective solution, demonstrates strong empirical results, and introduces a valuable benchmark dataset.  While some questions remain regarding its scalability and impact on a diverse range of users, the contribution is significant and impactful.
    *   The paper is not a complete game-changer but rather a strong incremental improvement that could guide others. The approach could have limited success in some spaces.
    *   The significance in results is present in the paper as it outperforms many other models, but the study needs to make sure it's a generalizable improvement.
    *   The impact is clear as the LLM does improve as a Judge.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### OPeRA: A Dataset of Observation, Persona, Rationale, and Action for Evaluating LLMs on Human Online Shopping Behavior Simulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05606v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyi Wang, Yuxuan Lu, Wenbo Li, Amirali Amini, Bo Sun, Yakov Bart, Weimin Lyu, Jiri Gesi, Tian Wang, Jing Huang, Yu Su, Upol Ehsan, Malihe Alikhani, Toby Jia-Jun Li, Lydia Chilton, Dakuo Wang
- **Abstract**: Can large language models (LLMs) accurately simulate the next web action of a specific user? While LLMs have shown promising capabilities in generating ``believable'' human behaviors, evaluating their ability to mimic real user behaviors remains an open challenge, largely due to the lack of high-quality, publicly available datasets that capture both the observable actions and the internal reasoning of an actual human user. To address this gap, we introduce OPERA, a novel dataset of Observation, Persona, Rationale, and Action collected from real human participants during online shopping sessions. OPERA is the first public dataset that comprehensively captures: user personas, browser observations, fine-grained web actions, and self-reported just-in-time rationales. We developed both an online questionnaire and a custom browser plugin to gather this dataset with high fidelity. Using OPERA, we establish the first benchmark to evaluate how well current LLMs can predict a specific user's next action and rationale with a given persona and <observation, action, rationale> history. This dataset lays the groundwork for future research into LLM agents that aim to act as personalized digital twins for human.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OPERA, a new dataset designed to facilitate research on human behavior simulation using large language models (LLMs) in the online shopping context. The dataset comprises user personas, web browser observations (HTML content and screenshots), fine-grained web actions, and self-reported just-in-time rationales for those actions. OPERA aims to address the limitations of existing datasets, which often lack detailed reasoning, persona information, or real-world user behavior data. The authors collected data from real human participants using a custom Chrome browser plugin called ShoppingFlow, and benchmarked the performance of several state-of-the-art LLMs on next-action prediction and joint action/rationale generation tasks. The results highlight that even advanced LLMs still struggle to accurately simulate complex personalized user behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in the creation and release of the OPERA dataset.  The combination of user personas, detailed action traces, rich HTML/screenshot context, and, critically, user-provided rationales is a significant step forward. Previous datasets typically lack this complete picture. While some components are individually present in other datasets (e.g., action traces, HTML structure), the integrated nature of OPERA makes it unique. Therefore, the claim of novelty is justified.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from several factors.  First, it addresses a critical gap in the field of LLM-based human behavior simulation, where the lack of high-quality, publicly available datasets hinders progress. OPERA's comprehensive nature should allow researchers to better evaluate and improve LLM agents in this domain. The choice of the online shopping domain is also smart: it’s a prevalent online activity with clear decision-making processes, making it a good testbed. Second, the benchmark experiments provide a valuable baseline for future research, demonstrating the current limitations of LLMs in accurately modeling user behavior. The ablation studies (removing persona and/or rationale) are also informative. The explicit capturing of rationale will likely be influential in future approaches to improving model performance for such tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:** The paper has some limitations. The dataset size, while substantial, is still relatively small compared to the scale of LLM training data. This could limit the ability to fine-tune very large models effectively. The focus on Amazon.com, while practical, limits the generalizability to other e-commerce platforms with different UI patterns.  The action space simplification is also a double-edged sword; while it makes evaluation more tractable, it also removes some of the richness of real user behavior (e.g., fine-grained mouse movements, precise scrolling).

*   **Impact:** The OPERA dataset has the potential to significantly influence research on LLM agents for personalized digital twins, UX testing, and potentially other areas like social science research. The availability of this dataset, and the benchmarks established within the paper, will likely spur innovation in this space. The dataset serves as a solid foundation for future research that aims to better understand and predict user behavior in online shopping scenarios, and potentially adapt and personalize services and interactions to match individual needs and preferences. The focus on rationales is particularly important for creating more interpretable and trustworthy AI systems.

*   **Rigor:** The data collection methodology seems sound, with efforts made to ensure data quality and protect user privacy. The post-processing steps (action space simplification, filtering) are clearly explained.  The benchmark experiments are well-defined, and the ablation studies provide further insights into the impact of different input factors. However, more extensive analysis of the dataset's characteristics (e.g., user demographics, action distributions) and a more thorough discussion of potential biases would further enhance the paper's rigor.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The OPERA dataset is a valuable and novel contribution to the field of LLM-based human behavior simulation. The comprehensiveness of the dataset, particularly the inclusion of user rationales, sets it apart from existing resources.  The benchmark experiments provide a useful starting point for future research. While the dataset size and focus on a single platform are limitations, the potential impact of OPERA on the field justifies a high score.  The benchmark itself is less groundbreaking, but useful as a test case for this new dataset.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Which Prompting Technique Should I Use? An Empirical Investigation of Prompting Techniques for Software Engineering Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05614v1)
- **Authors**: E. G. Santana Jr, Gabriel Benjamin, Melissa Araujo, Harrison Santos, David Freitas, Eduardo Almeida, Paulo Anselmo da M. S. Neto, Jiawei Li, Jina Chun, Iftekhar Ahmed
- **Abstract**: A growing variety of prompt engineering techniques has been proposed for Large Language Models (LLMs), yet systematic evaluation of each technique on individual software engineering (SE) tasks remains underexplored. In this study, we present a systematic evaluation of 14 established prompt techniques across 10 SE tasks using four LLM models. As identified in the prior literature, the selected prompting techniques span six core dimensions (Zero-Shot, Few-Shot, Thought Generation, Ensembling, Self-Criticism, and Decomposition). They are evaluated on tasks such as code generation, bug fixing, and code-oriented question answering, to name a few. Our results show which prompting techniques are most effective for SE tasks requiring complex logic and intensive reasoning versus those that rely more on contextual understanding and example-driven scenarios. We also analyze correlations between the linguistic characteristics of prompts and the factors that contribute to the effectiveness of prompting techniques in enhancing performance on SE tasks. Additionally, we report the time and token consumption for each prompting technique when applied to a specific task and model, offering guidance for practitioners in selecting the optimal prompting technique for their use cases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Which Prompting Technique Should I Use? An Empirical Investigation of Prompting Techniques for Software Engineering Tasks" presents a systematic evaluation of 14 established prompt engineering techniques across 10 software engineering (SE) tasks using four large language models (LLMs). It investigates how different prompting techniques impact performance, analyzes the association between linguistic features of prompts and performance, explores LLM-perceived factors contributing to effectiveness using contrastive explanations, and assesses the resource costs (token count and latency) associated with different techniques.  The goal is to provide guidance for practitioners in selecting optimal prompting techniques for specific SE use cases. The authors identified the most effective prompting techniques for tasks requiring complex logic and reasoning versus those based on contextual understanding. They then create composite prompts that integrate positively impactful techniques to observe further performance improvements, attempting to advance more robust and autonomous SE practices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its systematic and comparative evaluation of a wide range of prompt engineering techniques specifically applied to a variety of software engineering tasks. While individual prompting techniques are not new, the comprehensive benchmarking across different tasks, models, and performance metrics offers a more holistic view than previous studies. The investigation into linguistic features and LLM-based contrastive explanations adds another layer of novelty.  However, much of the techniques and tasks selected are based on previous work and serve primarily as the foundation for this comparative analysis. The key novelty is in the large-scale experimentation and analysis.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a crucial gap in the LLM literature, which is a lack of practical guidance on selecting appropriate prompting techniques for specific applications. By empirically comparing techniques and analyzing their characteristics, the authors provide valuable insights for software engineers and researchers. The results on resource costs are particularly useful for practical deployment scenarios. The study is made even more valuable by open sourcing their datasets, models, scripts, which can act as an excellent resource for future research. It can also encourage more consistent experimental design for the comparative analysis of LM-based approaches for SE tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study covers a wide range of prompt engineering techniques, SE tasks, and LLM models.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on practical guidance and resource costs makes the findings highly relevant for practitioners.
*   **In-depth Analysis:** The investigation into linguistic features and LLM-based explanations provides deeper insights into the effectiveness of prompting techniques.
*   **Open Source Contribution:**  Releasing the data and code promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Clear Identification of Factors:** The study successfully identifies key factors that correlate to good prompting strategies, notably the trade-offs between token efficiency and task complexity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** While the study uses a diverse set of LLMs, the results may not be fully generalizable to all models, especially those with significantly different architectures or training data.
*   **Task Selection:** Selection of tasks focuses primarily on code understanding and generation tasks. While this makes sense, there is a lack of higher-level abstraction tasks such as testing and architecture design.
*   **Dependence on Pre-existing Datasets:** The quality of results will inevitably be tied to the quality of the pre-existing datasets used, even with careful sampling.
*   **Subjectivity in Qualitative Analysis:** The manual evaluation of LLM-generated explanations and the interpretation of themes could introduce some level of subjectivity, though the use of multiple raters mitigate this threat.
*   **Limited Exploration of Composite Prompting:** The effort to formulate composite prompts is only briefly mentioned and could have been explored more deeply, since that is where the authors could have added the most value.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

*   Providing a benchmark for evaluating new prompting techniques in SE tasks.
*   Guiding practitioners in selecting appropriate techniques for specific use cases.
*   Inspiring further research into the linguistic features and underlying mechanisms of effective prompting.
*   Promoting the development of automated prompt engineering tools.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the intersection of LLMs and software engineering. Its systematic and practical approach addresses a pressing need for guidance in this rapidly evolving field. While the novelty of individual components may be limited, the integration of multiple analyses and the scope of the evaluation warrant a high score.  The open sourcing of the project significantly contributes to the reproducibility and reusability of the research. The identified weaknesses are mostly limitations inherent to the nature of empirical studies in this domain. The paper’s impact lies in the broad overview it provides for SE prompting tasks, which is a valuable contribution for software engineering practitioners looking to integrate LLMs into their workflows.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Toward Greater Autonomy in Materials Discovery Agents: Unifying Planning, Physics, and Scientists
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05616v1)
- **Authors**: Lianhao Zhou, Hongyi Ling, Keqiang Yan, Kaiji Zhao, Xiaoning Qian, Raymundo Arróyave, Xiaofeng Qian, Shuiwang Ji
- **Abstract**: We aim at designing language agents with greater autonomy for crystal materials discovery. While most of existing studies restrict the agents to perform specific tasks within predefined workflows, we aim to automate workflow planning given high-level goals and scientist intuition. To this end, we propose Materials Agent unifying Planning, Physics, and Scientists, known as MAPPS. MAPPS consists of a Workflow Planner, a Tool Code Generator, and a Scientific Mediator. The Workflow Planner uses large language models (LLMs) to generate structured and multi-step workflows. The Tool Code Generator synthesizes executable Python code for various tasks, including invoking a force field foundation model that encodes physics. The Scientific Mediator coordinates communications, facilitates scientist feedback, and ensures robustness through error reflection and recovery. By unifying planning, physics, and scientists, MAPPS enables flexible and reliable materials discovery with greater autonomy, achieving a five-fold improvement in stability, uniqueness, and novelty rates compared with prior generative models when evaluated on the MP-20 data. We provide extensive experiments across diverse tasks to show that MAPPS is a promising framework for autonomous materials discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAPPS (Materials Agent unifying Planning, Physics, and Scientists), a multi-agent framework designed to increase the autonomy of AI agents in crystal materials discovery.  MAPPS consists of three key components: a Workflow Planner (uses LLMs to generate multi-step workflows), a Tool Code Generator (synthesizes executable Python code for various tasks, including physics-based tools), and a Scientific Mediator (coordinates communications, facilitates scientist feedback, and ensures robustness). The paper demonstrates that MAPPS, by unifying planning, physics, and human expertise, achieves improved stability, uniqueness, and novelty rates in materials discovery compared to existing generative models, specifically on the MP-20 dataset. Extensive experiments are conducted across different materials discovery tasks, showcasing the potential of MAPPS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty in Agent Autonomy:** The core strength lies in moving beyond Level 1 autonomy (tool execution) to Level 2 (human-guided planning). This is a significant step, allowing agents to plan workflows independently rather than being confined to pre-defined pipelines. The framework's design for enabling this greater autonomy is well-structured.
    *   **Integration of Planning, Physics, and Human Expertise:** Unifying these three elements is a valuable approach.  The physics-informed aspect, through the Tool Code Generator, grounds the agents' actions in fundamental laws, enhancing reliability.  The human-in-the-loop mechanism, managed by the Scientific Mediator, mitigates risks and allows for expert guidance.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results across various tasks (crystal generation, structure prediction, property-guided generation) provide strong evidence for MAPPS's effectiveness. The reported five-fold improvement in stability, uniqueness, and novelty on MP-20 is compelling.
    *   **Framework Design:** The decomposition of the problem into Workflow Planner, Tool Code Generator, and Scientific Mediator allows for modularity and potential for future expansion.
    *   **Addressing limitations of prior agent systems:** Explicitly addresses the limitations of prior agent systems by surpassing them in autonomy and the ability to incorporate intuition and feedback from human experts.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:**  Like many current AI-for-science approaches, MAPPS depends heavily on the capabilities of LLMs. The paper acknowledges that LLMs' planning abilities are still limited and a topic of research. The success of MAPPS hinges on the continued development and refinement of LLMs. While this dependence is explicitly stated, it also underscores that the framework's broader applicability is tied to advances in foundation models.
    *   **Level 2 Autonomy Limitation:** Although the paper claims significant novelty in achieving level 2 autonomy, it is more an incremental advance in autonomy since the method still requires heavy human guidance.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While diverse, the experiments primarily focus on crystal materials discovery. The generalizability of MAPPS to other scientific domains (e.g., organic molecules, polymers) is not thoroughly explored.
    *   **Lack of Analysis on Failure Cases:** While the paper presents results of generated material, the generated structure lacks details.

*   **Significance:**
    *   MAPPS presents a valuable step towards more autonomous scientific discovery agents.  The framework's design and demonstrated performance contribute to the ongoing efforts to leverage AI for accelerating materials research.
    *   The focus on scientific workflow planning is a relevant and important area, particularly as the complexity of scientific problems increases.
    *   The emphasis on grounding agent actions in physical laws and incorporating human expertise addresses critical concerns about the reliability and validity of AI-driven scientific discovery.

**Overall Assessment:**

MAPPS is a well-designed and empirically validated framework that advances the state-of-the-art in autonomous materials discovery agents. Its strengths lie in its novel agent autonomy architecture, integration of planning, physics, and human knowledge, and solid experimental results. While the reliance on LLMs and the focus on crystal materials are limitations, the paper represents a significant contribution to the field of AI for science. It introduces a promising approach for enabling more flexible and reliable materials discovery.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The score reflects the paper's substantial contribution to the field. It clearly demonstrates a novel agent autonomy system with rigorous experimental results. The innovation lies in moving beyond simple tool execution and enabling agents to perform human-guided planning. However, the limitations described above, particularly the dependence on LLMs, and the fact that it is only a Level 2 autonomous system, prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper's specific focus on crystal materials also limits its broader impact, at least for now. Despite these limitations, the paper offers a valuable step forward and has the potential to influence future research in autonomous scientific discovery.

"

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Deployability-Centric Infrastructure-as-Code Generation: An LLM-based Iterative Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05623v1)
- **Authors**: Tianyi Zhang, Shidong Pan, Zejun Zhang, Zhenchang Xing, Xiaoyu Sun
- **Abstract**: Infrastructure-as-Code (IaC) generation holds significant promise for automating cloud infrastructure provisioning. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) present a promising opportunity to democratize IaC development by generating deployable infrastructure templates from natural language descriptions, but current evaluation focuses on syntactic correctness while ignoring deployability, the fatal measure of IaC template utility. We address this gap through two contributions: (1) IaCGen, an LLM-based deployability-centric framework that uses iterative feedback mechanism to generate IaC templates, and (2) DPIaC-Eval, a deployability-centric IaC template benchmark consists of 153 real-world scenarios that can evaluate syntax, deployment, user intent, and security. Our evaluation reveals that state-of-the-art LLMs initially performed poorly, with Claude-3.5 and Claude-3.7 achieving only 30.2% and 26.8% deployment success on the first attempt respectively. However, IaCGen transforms this performance dramatically: all evaluated models reach over 90% passItr@25, with Claude-3.5 and Claude-3.7 achieving 98% success rate. Despite these improvements, critical challenges remain in user intent alignment (25.2% accuracy) and security compliance (8.4% pass rate), highlighting areas requiring continued research. Our work provides the first comprehensive assessment of deployability-centric IaC template generation and establishes a foundation for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IaCGen, a novel framework for Infrastructure-as-Code (IaC) generation that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) and a deployability-centric iterative feedback mechanism.  It also presents DPIaC-Eval, a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate IaC template generation, focusing not only on syntactic correctness but also deployability, user intent matching, and security compliance. The authors evaluate IaCGen using several popular LLMs, demonstrating significant performance improvements compared to using LLMs directly.  The iterative feedback process, mirroring real-world DevOps workflows, allows the LLMs to learn from deployment failures and refine their templates.  The study identifies common error patterns and the impact of human-in-the-loop feedback, highlighting remaining challenges in areas like user intent alignment and security compliance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a significant and multi-faceted contribution to the field.
    *   The **IaCGen framework** is a novel approach. The iterative deployment-driven feedback mechanism is a substantial improvement over existing methods that focus primarily on syntactic correctness. This addresses a critical gap in applying LLMs to IaC, where practical utility depends on deployability.
    *   The **DPIaC-Eval benchmark** is a valuable contribution. Existing IaC benchmarks mainly evaluated syntactic correctness, while DPIaC-Eval also considers deployability, user intent and security. The lack of available comprehensive benchmarks covering various aspects of IaC quality has definitely been a roadblock.
    *   The comprehensive **empirical evaluation** of multiple LLMs using the new benchmark, along with the analysis of error patterns and the impact of human-in-the-loop feedback, provides valuable insights.

*   **Significance:** The work has considerable significance.
    *   It directly addresses a practical problem: the difficulty and expertise required for IaC development. By improving automated IaC generation, it democratizes cloud infrastructure management.
    *   The findings on LLM performance, error patterns, and the effectiveness of feedback mechanisms are crucial for guiding future research in this area.
    *   The DPIaC-Eval benchmark will likely become a standard resource for evaluating IaC generation methods, driving progress in the field.
    *   The research highlights the important, yet often overlooked, security aspect within IaC and will push it towards higher priority.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The problem is well-defined and highly relevant in the current cloud-centric landscape.
    *   The solution (IaCGen) is well-engineered and addresses the core challenges of IaC generation.
    *   The benchmark (DPIaC-Eval) is comprehensive, well-constructed, and focuses on critical real-world aspects of IaC.
    *   The experiments are thorough, using multiple LLMs and a controlled experimental setup.
    *   The analysis of the results is detailed, providing valuable insights into model performance, error patterns, and the impact of feedback.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly presents the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability Claim:** While the paper mentions generalization to Terraform, the evaluation is limited to syntactic validation. Deployability testing with Terraform, which has its own state management complexities, would further strengthen this claim.
    *   **Security Focus:** The security analysis, while a valuable addition, is limited to Checkov. The authors do not address the human-driven side of security and its impact on the overall outcome. The paper's findings suggest that an understanding of security vulnerabilities remains a crucial gap for current models, so an area to grow.
    *   **Benchmark Size:** Though a significant contribution, the DPIaC-Eval dataset could be even larger in the future to cover an even greater range of AWS services and complexity levels.

*   **Impact:** The paper has a high potential impact on the field.  It provides a practical framework, a valuable benchmark, and crucial insights that will likely inspire and guide future research in IaC generation.  The work can help developers more easily adopt and manage cloud infrastructure, ultimately accelerating software development and deployment.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper provides significant contributions to the field of LLM-based IaC generation. The introduction of IaCGen and DPIaC-Eval sets a new standard for evaluating and improving IaC template generation, moving beyond purely syntactic considerations to encompass deployability, user intent, and security. While there are minor limitations, the overall impact of the work is substantial, making it a significant contribution to the research field.

### Heterogeneous Sequel-Aware Graph Neural Networks for Sequential Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05625v1)
- **Authors**: Anushka Tiwari, Haimonti Dutta, Shahrzad Khanizadeh
- **Abstract**: Graph-based recommendation systems use higher-order user and item embeddings for next-item predictions. Dynamically adding collaborative signals from neighbors helps to use similar users' preferences during learning. While item-item correlations and their impact on recommendations have been studied, the efficacy of temporal item sequences for recommendations is much less explored. In this paper, we examine temporal item sequence (sequel-aware) embeddings along with higher-order user embeddings and show that sequel-aware Graph Neural Networks have better (or comparable) recommendation performance than graph-based recommendation systems that do not consider sequel information. Extensive empirical results comparing Heterogeneous Sequel-aware Graph Neural Networks (HSAL-GNNs) to other algorithms for sequential learning (such as transformers, graph neural networks, auto-encoders) are presented on three synthetic and three real-world datasets. Our results indicate that the incorporation of sequence information from items greatly enhances recommendations.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Heterogeneous Sequel-Aware Graph Neural Networks (HSAL-GNNs) for sequential recommendation, aiming to improve prediction accuracy by incorporating temporal item sequences (sequel information) alongside higher-order user embeddings. The core idea is to leverage the relationships between items in a sequence (e.g., movies in a series) to better model user preferences and predict the next item a user might interact with. The paper presents an algorithm for constructing a sequel-aware dynamic graph, incorporating both user-item interactions and sequel dependencies. The HSAL-GNN architecture uses a two-level information propagation mechanism: sequel-aware item-item interactions and user-item interactions. Empirical results on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that HSAL-GNNs achieve better or comparable performance compared to existing graph-based recommendation systems that do not explicitly consider sequel information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in explicitly modeling and incorporating sequel-aware information in a GNN-based sequential recommendation system. While existing works have explored user-item interactions and item representations, they often overlook the temporal relationships between items within a series. Addressing this gap is a valuable contribution. However, the GNN architecture itself does not present substantial algorithmic novelty; its main contribution arises from the intelligent crafting of the input graph and the embedding of temporal sequencing information. The fusion methods and the adoption of an already tested heterogenous GNN do not bring much novelty either.

**Significance:** The significance of this work is its demonstration that incorporating sequel information can improve recommendation performance, especially in datasets where sequential dependencies are present. This finding has practical implications for real-world recommendation systems in domains like movies, books, and other media. The paper provides empirical evidence to support this claim through comprehensive experiments across various datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   The paper addresses a relevant and practical problem in sequential recommendation.
*   The proposed HSAL-GNN architecture effectively incorporates sequel-aware information.
*   The empirical results demonstrate improved performance compared to existing baselines on datasets with sequel dependencies.
*   The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of different components of the HSAL-GNN.
*   The parameter sensitivity analysis is valuable for understanding model behavior.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The GNN architecture itself does not offer significant algorithmic innovation; most improvements come from data representation and embedding.
*   The improvement in some datasets, like ML-1m and ML-100k is only marginal, therefore the benefit might not warrant the effort.
*   The method has limited use in data scarce settings.
*   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the computational complexity.
*   The improvement is not consistent across datasets; more analysis is needed to understand dataset characteristics where the proposed method performs best.
*   The novelty of the method could have been improved by the exploration of more complex GNN architectures.
*   There may be simpler methods with comparable performance, which is not addressed.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a useful extension to sequential recommendation systems by considering temporal sequences of items. The implementation and evaluation are sound. However, the algorithmic novelty is somewhat limited, as it primarily builds upon existing GNN architectures and focuses on crafting the input graph to represent sequential relationships. The degree of improvement is highly data-dependent. The significance is thus somewhat constrained by the lack of major algorithmic breakthroughs and the variable performance across different datasets.

### Leveraging Self-Attention for Input-Dependent Soft Prompting in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05629v1)
- **Authors**: Ananth Muppidi, Abhilash Nandy, Sambaran Bandyopadhyay
- **Abstract**: The performance of large language models in domain-specific tasks necessitates fine-tuning, which is computationally expensive and technically challenging. This paper focuses on parameter-efficient fine-tuning using soft prompting, a promising approach that adapts pre-trained models to downstream tasks by learning a small set of parameters. We propose a novel Input Dependent Soft Prompting technique with a self-Attention Mechanism (ID-SPAM) that generates soft prompts based on the input tokens and attends different tokens with varying importance. Our method is simple and efficient, keeping the number of trainable parameters small. We show the merits of the proposed approach compared to state-of-the-art techniques on various tasks and show the improved zero shot domain transfer capability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique called Input-Dependent Soft Prompting with a self-Attention Mechanism (ID-SPAM) for large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to generate soft prompts based on the input tokens, with a self-attention mechanism allowing the model to attend to different tokens with varying importance.  This approach aims to address limitations in existing soft prompting methods, such as input independence and increased trainable parameters. The paper demonstrates ID-SPAM's performance on various GLUE and SuperGLUE tasks, as well as in zero-shot domain transfer scenarios, comparing it against other parameter-efficient fine-tuning baselines like Prompt Tuning, P-Tuning, LORA, DEPT and SMOP. The results suggest that ID-SPAM achieves competitive or superior performance while maintaining a small number of trainable parameters and demonstrating good zero-shot transfer capability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the input-dependent soft prompt generation using a self-attention mechanism.  While input-dependent soft prompting is not entirely new, the specific implementation using self-attention to weigh the importance of different input tokens and then use the output to generate soft prompts is a distinguishing feature. The two-layer MLP in ID-SPAM is context-rich and more efficient in generating the soft prompt. Furthermore, their soft prompts can be prepended at any transformer layer.
