### Latent Diffusion Model Based Denoising Receiver for 6G Semantic Communication: From Stochastic Differential Theory to Application
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05710v1)
- **Authors**: Xiucheng Wang, Honggang Jia, Nan Cheng, Dusit Niyato
- **Abstract**: In this paper, a novel semantic communication framework empowered by generative artificial intelligence (GAI) is proposed, specifically leveraging the capabilities of diffusion models (DMs). A rigorous theoretical foundation is established based on stochastic differential equations (SDEs), which elucidates the denoising properties of DMs in mitigating additive white Gaussian noise (AWGN) in latent semantic representations. Crucially, a closed-form analytical relationship between the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) and the denoising timestep is derived, enabling the optimal selection of diffusion parameters for any given channel condition. To address the distribution mismatch between the received signal and the DM's training data, a mathematically principled scaling mechanism is introduced, ensuring robust performance across a wide range of SNRs without requiring model fine-tuning. Built upon this theoretical insight, we develop a latent diffusion model (LDM)-based semantic transceiver, wherein a variational autoencoder (VAE) is employed for efficient semantic compression, and a pretrained DM serves as a universal denoiser. Notably, the proposed architecture is fully training-free at inference time, offering high modularity and compatibility with large-scale pretrained LDMs. This design inherently supports zero-shot generalization and mitigates the challenges posed by out-of-distribution inputs. Extensive experimental evaluations demonstrate that the proposed framework significantly outperforms conventional neural-network-based semantic communication baselines, particularly under low SNR conditions and distributional shifts, thereby establishing a promising direction for GAI-driven robust semantic transmission in future 6G systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel semantic communication framework that leverages Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs) for denoising in the latent space of a Variational Autoencoder (VAE). It establishes a theoretical foundation based on Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs) to derive a closed-form relationship between the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) and the optimal denoising timestep in the LDM. This allows for adaptive selection of diffusion parameters based on channel conditions.  A mathematically principled scaling mechanism addresses the distribution mismatch between received signals and the DM's training data, enhancing robustness across a wide range of SNRs without requiring model fine-tuning. The proposed architecture is fully training-free at inference time, offering high modularity and compatibility with pre-trained LDMs. Experimental results demonstrate significant performance improvements over conventional neural network-based semantic communication baselines, particularly under low SNR conditions and distributional shifts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents several novel aspects:

*   **SDE-based Theoretical Foundation:** Establishing a rigorous theoretical link between SDEs, diffusion models, and semantic communication is a significant contribution. The closed-form derivation connecting SNR and denoising timestep provides a principled way to adapt the denoising process to channel conditions. This is not simply applying a known technique; it's a deeper theoretical understanding of *why* and *how* diffusion models can be effectively used in this context.
*   **Adaptive Scaling Mechanism:** Addressing the distribution mismatch issue with a mathematically principled scaling mechanism is crucial for robust performance, especially under OOD scenarios. Many semantic communication papers overlook this important practical challenge.
*   **Training-Free Inference & Modular Architecture:** The modular design that allows for plug-and-play compatibility with pre-trained LDMs is a significant advantage.  This avoids the need for task-specific fine-tuning and enables leveraging advancements in generative models.  The zero-shot generalization capabilities are also quite promising.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing Key Limitations:** The paper directly tackles the well-known robustness and generalization limitations of existing neural network-based semantic communication systems.  This is a critical step towards practical deployment.
*   **Improved Performance:** The demonstrated performance gains, especially under low SNR and distributional shifts, are substantial and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
*   **Potential Impact:** The framework provides a new direction for research in GAI-driven semantic communication, offering a promising path towards robust and scalable 6G systems. The theoretical insights could influence the design of future semantic communication architectures.

**Strengths:**

*   Strong theoretical grounding and mathematical derivations.
*   Addresses a critical problem in semantic communication (robustness to noise and OOD data).
*   Practical and modular architecture with training-free inference.
*   Extensive experimental validation and clear performance improvements.
*   Clear writing and presentation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The paper uses CelebA-HQ which while suitable for the task might be too simplistic. Testing on more diverse and challenging datasets could provide even stronger evidence for the framework's generalization capabilities.
*   While the framework *leverages* pretrained diffusion models, it doesn't fundamentally *improve* diffusion model technology itself. Its primary contribution is in *integrating* diffusion models into a semantic communication system and deriving theoretical results specific to that application.
*   The computational complexity of LDMs, even in latent space, can be substantial. The paper only briefly mentions low-latency operation and could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational overhead and potential optimization strategies. This aspect needs to be considered for real-time 6G application scenarios.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **8**. The paper offers a significant contribution to the field of semantic communication by providing a theoretically grounded and practically effective framework for robust denoising using diffusion models. While the framework's novelty lies primarily in the integration and adaptation of existing technologies, the theoretical analysis, adaptive scaling mechanism, and demonstration of substantial performance improvements justify a high score. The limitations mentioned above, while important, do not detract significantly from the overall contribution.  The potential impact on future 6G systems is substantial.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Large Language Models are Good Relational Learners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05725v1)
- **Authors**: Fang Wu, Vijay Prakash Dwivedi, Jure Leskovec
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across various domains, yet their application to relational deep learning (RDL) remains underexplored. Existing approaches adapt LLMs by traversing relational links between entities in a database and converting the structured data into flat text documents. Still, this text-based serialization disregards critical relational structures, introduces redundancy, and often exceeds standard LLM context lengths. We introduce Rel-LLM, a novel architecture that utilizes a graph neural network (GNN)- based encoder to generate structured relational prompts for LLMs within a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework. Unlike traditional text-based serialization approaches, our method preserves the inherent relational structure of databases while enabling LLMs to effectively process and reason over complex entity relationships. Specifically, the GNN encoder extracts a local subgraph around an entity to build feature representations that contain relevant entity relationships and temporal dependencies. These representations are transformed into structured prompts using a denormalization process, effectively allowing the LLM to reason over relational structures. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that Rel-LLM outperforms existing methods on key RDL tasks, offering a scalable and efficient approach to integrating LLMs with structured data sources. Code is available at https://github.com/smiles724/Rel-LLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Rel-LLM, a novel architecture that combines Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with Large Language Models (LLMs) for relational deep learning (RDL). It addresses the limitations of existing approaches that serialize relational data into flat text, which disregards inherent relational structures and can lead to redundancy and excessive context lengths. Rel-LLM uses a GNN-based encoder to generate structured relational prompts for LLMs within a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework. It preserves relational structure, allowing LLMs to reason effectively over complex entity relationships. The GNN encoder extracts local subgraphs to build feature representations, which are then transformed into structured prompts via a denormalization process. Experiments on benchmark RDL datasets demonstrate that Rel-LLM outperforms existing methods in predictive accuracy, relational reasoning, and temporal consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonable degree of novelty. While using GNNs and LLMs separately for various tasks is common, the specific integration of a GNN-based encoder to create structured relational prompts for an LLM, especially within a RAG framework tailored for relational databases, represents a novel architectural contribution. The temporal-aware subgraph sampling and the pretraining objective further enhance the architecture's capabilities. The core idea of maintaining structured data is significant.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the application of LLMs to relational data, which is a crucial and often under-explored area. The claimed improvements in predictive accuracy, relational reasoning, and temporal consistency, if robustly validated, contribute significantly to the field of relational deep learning. The potential of Rel-LLM to scale efficiently and effectively integrate LLMs with structured data sources is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a significant limitation in existing approaches to RDL, namely the loss of relational structure when data is serialized for LLMs.
    *   **Proposed Solution:** Rel-LLM provides a plausible and innovative solution that leverages the strengths of both GNNs and LLMs.
    *   **Experimental Results:**  The paper presents experimental results demonstrating the superiority of Rel-LLM over baseline methods on established RDL benchmarks.
    *   **Well-structured:** The architecture description is well-detailed, making it easy for the research community to understand and possibly replicate the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Insight:** There are too few insights regarding the performance of the LLM regarding relational tasks. In particular, the LLM might lack a specific bias regarding structured data.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper could strengthen validation by discussing some limitations of the architecture.
    *   **Ablation Study Details:** Further description is necessary regarding the ablation studies and how some elements are key regarding performance.
    *   **Dependency on Graph Structure:** The paper could potentially benefit from some experiments involving graph data augmentation.

*   **Potential Impact:** The approach of using a GNN encoder to generate prompts could influence future work in integrating LLMs with other structured data sources beyond relational databases.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 6/10 for the following reasons:

1.  The core idea of combining GNNs and LLMs for RDL with structured prompts demonstrates novelty. However, there's little evidence showing that the LLM by itself might require further structure to perform well regarding relational data.
2.  The experimental results on standard RDL benchmarks support the claims of superior performance over existing methods. The paper could benefit from a discussion of the architectural design choices.
3.  The paper could have better detailed some limitations and insights from the experimental results. More particularly, the architectural design.

Despite these weaknesses, the paper presents a solid contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research in integrating LLMs with structured data.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Do LLMs Really Forget? Evaluating Unlearning with Knowledge Correlation and Confidence Awareness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05735v1)
- **Authors**: Rongzhe Wei, Peizhi Niu, Hans Hao-Hsun Hsu, Ruihan Wu, Haoteng Yin, Mohsen Ghassemi, Yifan Li, Vamsi K. Potluru, Eli Chien, Kamalika Chaudhuri, Olgica Milenkovic, Pan Li
- **Abstract**: Machine unlearning techniques aim to mitigate unintended memorization in large language models (LLMs). However, existing approaches predominantly focus on the explicit removal of isolated facts, often overlooking latent inferential dependencies and the non-deterministic nature of knowledge within LLMs. Consequently, facts presumed forgotten may persist implicitly through correlated information. To address these challenges, we propose a knowledge unlearning evaluation framework that more accurately captures the implicit structure of real-world knowledge by representing relevant factual contexts as knowledge graphs with associated confidence scores. We further develop an inference-based evaluation protocol leveraging powerful LLMs as judges; these judges reason over the extracted knowledge subgraph to determine unlearning success. Our LLM judges utilize carefully designed prompts and are calibrated against human evaluations to ensure their trustworthiness and stability. Extensive experiments on our newly constructed benchmark demonstrate that our framework provides a more realistic and rigorous assessment of unlearning performance. Moreover, our findings reveal that current evaluation strategies tend to overestimate unlearning effectiveness. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Graph-COM/Knowledge_Unlearning.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current machine unlearning evaluation methods for large language models (LLMs). It argues that existing approaches often focus on the explicit removal of isolated facts, neglecting the latent inferential dependencies and non-deterministic nature of knowledge within LLMs. The authors propose a new evaluation framework that represents relevant factual contexts as knowledge graphs with confidence scores. They use LLMs as judges to reason over extracted knowledge subgraphs to determine unlearning success, calibrating the LLM judges against human evaluations. The experiments on a new benchmark demonstrate that the proposed framework provides a more realistic assessment of unlearning performance, revealing that current strategies tend to overestimate unlearning effectiveness. The code and the benchmark are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to evaluating machine unlearning in LLMs. The key innovations include:

    *   Representing knowledge as confidence-aware knowledge graphs to capture implicit relationships.
    *   Using LLMs as judges for inference-based evaluation.
    *   A new benchmark specifically designed to test unlearning effectiveness considering knowledge interdependencies.
    *   Calibration of LLM judges with human expert judgments.

    These aspects significantly improve upon existing methods that primarily focus on isolated fact removal. The concept of leveraging LLMs to judge knowledge unlearning is intriguing and offers a scalable solution to a complex problem. Prior work has explored multi-fact interactions, but this paper takes a significant leap by using LLMs to approximate the human reasoning that is used to deduce implied knowledge from relationships between facts.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight a critical weakness in current unlearning evaluation strategies. By demonstrating that current methods overestimate unlearning effectiveness, the authors raise awareness about the need for more robust evaluation approaches that account for knowledge correlations. This has implications for the design and development of future unlearning techniques. The release of the benchmark and evaluation protocol further enhances the paper's potential impact, enabling other researchers to build upon this work. The work has potential in downstream applications of knowledge unlearning such as protecting users' privacy and removing false information.

*   **Strengths:**
    * Rigorous methodology: The proposed framework is well-defined, thoroughly explained, and supported by extensive experiments.
    * Extensive analysis: The authors present a detailed analysis of the experimental results, providing valuable insights into the limitations of existing unlearning methods and the effectiveness of their framework. The ablation studies of the models are particularly insightful.
    * Validation: The use of human evaluations to calibrate the LLM judges increases the reliability and trustworthiness of the results.
    * Comprehensive dataset: The construction and utilization of YAGO3-10 allows for reliable construction of the graph knowledge.
    * Open-source resources: The release of the code and benchmark promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * The reliance on external knowledge graphs (e.g., Wikidata) for constructing supporting subgraphs may limit the framework's ability to capture all latent knowledge within LLMs, especially facts that are not well-represented in these external sources. While the authors acknowledge this limitation, it is a fundamental constraint of the approach. The work notes this limitation but does not seek to address it.
    * The reliance on GPT-4-mini may not scale effectively to larger-scale knowledge unlearning tasks.
    * There appears to be an over-reliance on heuristics when reasoning about knowledge in the generated graphs - it is not guaranteed that the models used truly capture human understanding and relationships.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of machine unlearning by:

    *   Shifting the focus from isolated fact removal to knowledge correlation awareness.
    *   Promoting the use of inference-based evaluation methods.
    *   Providing a valuable benchmark for comparing the performance of different unlearning techniques.
    *   Encouraging the development of more robust and effective unlearning algorithms.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty and significance of the work, the thorough methodology, comprehensive analysis, and potential impact on the field, a high score is warranted. However, the limitations regarding the reliance on external knowledge graphs and potentially computationally-intensive reliance on LLMs for judging inferrability, prevent it from being a perfect score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLM-Symbolic Integration for Robust Temporal Tabular Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05746v1)
- **Authors**: Atharv Kulkarni, Kushagra Dixit, Vivek Srikumar, Dan Roth, Vivek Gupta
- **Abstract**: Temporal tabular question answering presents a significant challenge for Large Language Models (LLMs), requiring robust reasoning over structured data, which is a task where traditional prompting methods often fall short. These methods face challenges such as memorization, sensitivity to table size, and reduced performance on complex queries. To overcome these limitations, we introduce TempTabQA-C, a synthetic dataset designed for systematic and controlled evaluations, alongside a symbolic intermediate representation that transforms tables into database schemas. This structured approach allows LLMs to generate and execute SQL queries, enhancing generalization and mitigating biases. By incorporating adaptive few-shot prompting with contextually tailored examples, our method achieves superior robustness, scalability, and performance. Experimental results consistently highlight improvements across key challenges, setting a new benchmark for robust temporal reasoning with LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TEMPTABQA-C, a synthetically generated dataset and associated framework designed to enhance temporal tabular reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of current prompting methods (memorization, sensitivity to table size, and performance on complex queries) by incorporating a symbolic intermediate representation.  This representation transforms tables into database schemas, enabling LLMs to generate and execute SQL queries. The framework also uses adaptive few-shot prompting, dynamically selecting relevant examples. The paper demonstrates the framework's improved robustness, scalability, and performance on tasks like counterfactual reasoning compared to direct prompting approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a significant limitation:** The paper directly tackles the problem of robust and scalable temporal reasoning with LLMs, an area where existing methods often struggle.
*   **Novel Dataset:** The creation of TEMPTABQA-C is a substantial contribution. The synthetic generation provides controlled variation in data characteristics, enabling systematic evaluation and analysis of LLM capabilities. The control over various aspects of the dataset including table size, question complexity, and counterfactual scenarios, allows researchers to precisely diagnose weaknesses.
*   **Symbolic Intermediate Representation:** The use of SQL as an intermediate representation is a good way to promote more robust and interpretable reasoning.  Transforming unstructured data into a structured schema allows for leveraging the power of database querying, mitigating reliance on memorization and biases. The use of SQL is a structured and verifiable reasoning path for evaluating and improving the model's accuracy.
*   **Adaptive Few-Shot Prompting:**  Dynamically selecting relevant examples is an advancement over static few-shot prompting, enhancing flexibility and performance across diverse scenarios.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental setup covers critical aspects like counterfactual robustness, scalability, and question complexity, providing a thorough analysis of the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Data Limitation:** While the controlled generation of TEMPTABQA-C is a strength, it's a synthetic dataset. There's a risk that models trained or evaluated on TEMPTABQA-C might not generalize well to real-world tabular data with its inherent noise, inconsistencies, and complexities.
*   **SQL Generation Dependency:** The framework's performance depends on the LLM's ability to accurately translate natural language questions into correct SQL queries. Errors in SQL generation can propagate through the entire pipeline, leading to incorrect answers. The paper acknowledges this limitation but could provide more details on the types of SQL errors encountered and strategies for mitigating them.
*   **Lack of Novelty in using SQL:** the novelty of the paper in using SQL is questionable. the paper seems to highlight only that this is a viable option without expanding on any new advances made in the SQL generation field. It would increase its significance by focusing more on the contribution with respect to the SQL framework.
*   **Limited Benchmarking:** While the results are compelling within the context of the paper, comparing to a wider range of state-of-the-art tabular QA methods would strengthen the claims.
*   **Potential for Overfitting on the Schema:** Even with masking the raw data, reliance on a fixed schema could still introduce biases if the LLM learns to exploit particular patterns within the schema design. This concern can be reduced by considering multiple schemas.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a synthetic dataset with diverse scenarios and controllable characteristics for temporal tabular reasoning. The proposed symbolic intermediate representation approach offers a promising avenue for improving robustness, interpretability, and scalability compared to direct prompting methods. The work establishes a new benchmark, encourages a move towards structured reasoning, and facilitates more in-depth analysis of LLM capabilities in this challenging area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a practical solution to a key issue in LLM tabular reasoning, specifically for temporal questions, and introduces an innovative framework and a valuable dataset. The improvement in robust reasoning with its ability to outperform memorization, and to adapt to varying datasets, makes the work practical. However, the paper needs to improve its SQL generation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Writing-RL: Advancing Long-form Writing via Adaptive Curriculum Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05760v1)
- **Authors**: Xuanyu Lei, Chenliang Li, Yuning Wu, Kaiming Liu, Weizhou Shen, Peng Li, Ming Yan, Ji Zhang, Fei Huang, Yang Liu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have enabled strong performance in long-form writing, yet existing supervised fine-tuning (SFT) approaches suffer from limitations such as data saturation and restricted learning capacity bounded by teacher signals. In this work, we present Writing-RL: an Adaptive Curriculum Reinforcement Learning framework to advance long-form writing capabilities beyond SFT. The framework consists of three key components: Margin-aware Data Selection strategy that prioritizes samples with high learning potential, Pairwise Comparison Reward mechanism that provides discriminative learning signals in the absence of verifiable rewards, and Dynamic Reference Scheduling approach, which plays a particularly critical role by adaptively adjusting task difficulty based on evolving model performance. Experiments on 7B-scale writer models show that our RL framework largely improves long-form writing performance over strong SFT baselines. Furthermore, we observe that models trained with long-output RL generalize surprisingly well to long-input reasoning tasks, potentially offering a promising perspective for rethinking long-context training.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, adhering to your requested rigor and emphasis on justification:

**Summary:**

The paper presents Writing-RL, a novel Adaptive Curriculum Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework designed to advance long-form writing capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs) beyond the limitations of Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT). The framework comprises three key components: (1) Margin-aware Data Selection, prioritizing training samples based on the performance gap between the policy model and the best competing model; (2) Pairwise Comparison Reward, providing more granular learning signals by challenging the model to surpass high-quality reference outputs; and (3) Dynamic Reference Scheduling, adaptively adjusting the task difficulty based on the model's evolving competence. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of Writing-RL on 7B-scale writer models, showing significant improvements in long-form writing performance and intriguing generalization to long-input reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant challenge in the field of LLMs: how to improve long-form writing capabilities beyond the constraints of SFT, particularly in the absence of readily available ground truths. The core strength of the paper lies in its well-motivated and thoughtfully designed RL framework. The three components work synergistically to create a robust learning environment:

*   **Margin-aware Data Selection:** This is a novel approach that moves beyond simply selecting the "hardest" examples. By focusing on the gap between the policy model and stronger competitors, it optimizes for areas where the model has the most potential for improvement.

*   **Pairwise Comparison Reward:** Addressing the lack of verifiable rewards in long-form writing is crucial. The pairwise comparison provides a more nuanced signal than simple pointwise scoring, encouraging the model to generate outputs of demonstrably higher quality.

*   **Dynamic Reference Scheduling:** This is perhaps the most innovative aspect. By adaptively adjusting the difficulty of the learning task based on the model's evolving abilities, it maintains consistent learning pressure and avoids data saturation.

The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating substantial gains over strong SFT baselines on established benchmarks. The finding that RL-trained writer models generalize to long-input reasoning tasks is particularly intriguing and suggests a potentially novel perspective on long-context training.

However, the paper also has weaknesses:

*   **Limited Model Scale:** While the results on 7B-scale models are promising, it remains to be seen how well the framework scales to larger, more powerful LLMs.

*   **Judge Model Dependency:** The framework relies on the quality and impartiality of the LLM-based judge (Qwen-Plus in this case). Any biases or inconsistencies in the judge could negatively impact the training process.

*   **Exploration of Alternatives:** It would be beneficial to see a more thorough comparison with alternative RL approaches for long-form generation, if any exist. While the lack of prior work highlights the novelty, a discussion of potential alternative designs would strengthen the paper.

*   **Reasoning Generalization:** The reasoning generalization result is interesting, but requires further investigation to understand why training on long outputs leads to reasoning improvements.  More controlled experiments to disentangle the factors at play would be beneficial.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by demonstrating a practical and effective RL framework for long-form writing. The framework's modular design and adaptive nature make it potentially applicable to other generative tasks where ground truths are scarce. The intriguing generalization results also open up new avenues for research into the relationship between long-context understanding and generation.

**Score:**

Based on the novelty, significance, and quality of the work, I assign a score of **8**. The paper presents a well-designed and validated RL framework that addresses a key challenge in LLM research. While there are some limitations and areas for future exploration, the paper's strengths significantly outweigh its weaknesses.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### BiTrajDiff: Bidirectional Trajectory Generation with Diffusion Models for Offline Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05762v1)
- **Authors**: Yunpeng Qing, Shuo Chen, Yixiao Chi, Shunyu Liu, Sixu Lin, Changqing Zou
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in offline Reinforcement Learning (RL) have proven that effective policy learning can benefit from imposing conservative constraints on pre-collected datasets. However, such static datasets often exhibit distribution bias, resulting in limited generalizability. To address this limitation, a straightforward solution is data augmentation (DA), which leverages generative models to enrich data distribution. Despite the promising results, current DA techniques focus solely on reconstructing future trajectories from given states, while ignoring the exploration of history transitions that reach them. This single-direction paradigm inevitably hinders the discovery of diverse behavior patterns, especially those leading to critical states that may have yielded high-reward outcomes. In this work, we introduce Bidirectional Trajectory Diffusion (BiTrajDiff), a novel DA framework for offline RL that models both future and history trajectories from any intermediate states. Specifically, we decompose the trajectory generation task into two independent yet complementary diffusion processes: one generating forward trajectories to predict future dynamics, and the other generating backward trajectories to trace essential history transitions.BiTrajDiff can efficiently leverage critical states as anchors to expand into potentially valuable yet underexplored regions of the state space, thereby facilitating dataset diversity. Extensive experiments on the D4RL benchmark suite demonstrate that BiTrajDiff achieves superior performance compared to other advanced DA methods across various offline RL backbones.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the BiTrajDiff paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BiTrajDiff, a novel data augmentation (DA) framework for offline reinforcement learning (RL). Unlike existing DA methods that focus solely on generating future trajectories from a given state, BiTrajDiff models both future and *history* trajectories from an intermediate state. This is achieved using two diffusion models: one generates forward trajectories, and the other generates backward trajectories. By stitching these trajectories together at the intermediate state, BiTrajDiff aims to expand the diversity of the dataset and facilitate the discovery of better policies by bridging states that are not directly connected in the original data. The method includes an inverse dynamics model and a reward model for completing the augmented trajectories, as well as a filtering mechanism to remove out-of-distribution and suboptimal trajectories. Experiments on the D4RL benchmark demonstrate that BiTrajDiff outperforms existing DA methods across various offline RL algorithms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of generating *both* forward and backward trajectories from an intermediate state is a relatively novel approach in the context of data augmentation for offline RL.  While some prior work has explored reverse trajectories (e.g., for imitation learning or trajectory stitching), the concurrent, bidirectional generation specifically for DA is a distinguishing feature. The stitching mechanism is also somewhat novel although not radically groundbreaking. The use of diffusion models for trajectory generation is not new in itself but the bidirectional approach is.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the potential to address a key limitation of offline RL: the distribution shift between the offline dataset and the learned policy. By augmenting the dataset with trajectories that explore more broadly, BiTrajDiff offers a way to improve the generalizability and performance of offline RL algorithms. The empirical results on D4RL support this claim, showing consistent improvements over existing DA methods and standard offline RL baselines. This may allow the field to unlock more real world applications because of this increased robustness.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a clear limitation of existing DA methods in offline RL.
    *   The BiTrajDiff framework is well-motivated and technically sound.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive and demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach across a range of environments and algorithms.
    *   The ablation studies provides some insights into how the framework enhances performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The complexity of the framework (two diffusion models, inverse dynamics model, reward model, filtering mechanism) might be a barrier to entry for some researchers, though the authors have stated they are releasing their code to ameliorate this concern.
    *   The justification for some design choices (e.g., the specific filtering mechanism) could be stronger.  More analysis of how different hyperparameter choices impact the generated trajectories would be useful.
    *   While the paper shows consistent improvements, the performance gains are not always dramatic. Some environments (like some of the `expert` datasets) see smaller gains, suggesting that the benefits of increased exploration are less pronounced when the original dataset is already of high quality.
    *   The paper makes some statements of the superiority of the method without any comparison to other potential DA schemes, or explanation for why certain approaches were not chosen.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the BiTrajDiff framework proves to be robust and scalable to more complex environments, it could become a valuable tool for improving the performance of offline RL algorithms. Its emphasis on bridging previously disconnected states may prove particularly useful in sparse-reward environments or in settings where exploration is costly. This might shift data augmentation for Offline RL away from a one directional to a more two-directional method of creating more complete datasets.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

BiTrajDiff introduces a novel and potentially impactful approach to data augmentation in offline RL. The bidirectional trajectory generation is a creative solution to the limitations of single-direction DA methods. The empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of the method. However, the complexity of the framework, the incremental nature of the performance gains, and the limited amount of comparative analysis with other potential DA schemes prevents it from receiving a higher score. Overall it has good novelty, and potential for large impact if it continues to be developed, so merits a 7.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### BioMol-MQA: A Multi-Modal Question Answering Dataset For LLM Reasoning Over Bio-Molecular Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05766v1)
- **Authors**: Saptarshi Sengupta, Shuhua Yang, Paul Kwong Yu, Fali Wang, Suhang Wang
- **Abstract**: Retrieval augmented generation (RAG) has shown great power in improving Large Language Models (LLMs). However, most existing RAG-based LLMs are dedicated to retrieving single modality information, mainly text; while for many real-world problems, such as healthcare, information relevant to queries can manifest in various modalities such as knowledge graph, text (clinical notes), and complex molecular structure. Thus, being able to retrieve relevant multi-modality domain-specific information, and reason and synthesize diverse knowledge to generate an accurate response is important. To address the gap, we present BioMol-MQA, a new question-answering (QA) dataset on polypharmacy, which is composed of two parts (i) a multimodal knowledge graph (KG) with text and molecular structure for information retrieval; and (ii) challenging questions that designed to test LLM capabilities in retrieving and reasoning over multimodal KG to answer questions. Our benchmarks indicate that existing LLMs struggle to answer these questions and do well only when given the necessary background data, signaling the necessity for strong RAG frameworks.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BioMol-MQA, a new multi-modal question-answering dataset designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to reason over complex bio-molecular interactions. The dataset focuses on polypharmacy, a healthcare issue involving concurrent use of multiple medications. BioMol-MQA consists of (i) a multimodal knowledge graph combining text, molecular structure (SMILES strings), and drug/protein interaction data, and (ii) challenging questions that require LLMs to retrieve and reason over information from these modalities to produce accurate answers. The paper details the dataset construction process, including data acquisition, text post-processing, molecular interaction extraction, question generation, and evaluation.  The authors benchmark several existing LLMs on the dataset, demonstrating that current LLMs struggle to answer the questions effectively without being provided relevant context. Experiments also explore retrieval performance using text-based, graph-based, and hybrid retrieval methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the construction of a multi-modal QA dataset specifically focused on bio-molecular interactions and polypharmacy. Existing datasets tend to focus on single modalities or general knowledge, while BioMol-MQA tackles a more complex, domain-specific problem. The integration of molecular structure (SMILES) as a retrieval modality is also a relatively novel aspect. The synthetic data augmentation pipeline, which includes using an LLM to augment the knowledge graph with molecular interactions and create challenging questions, is also a significant contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of LLMs for real-world, high-stakes applications like healthcare. Demonstrating the need for strong RAG frameworks capable of retrieving and reasoning over multi-modal domain-specific knowledge has high impact. The availability of BioMol-MQA as a benchmark should stimulate research into more effective multi-modal RAG methods. The inclusion of a dataset split dedicated to fine-tuning is another key consideration. The dataset has the potential to: (i) improve the capabilities of current LLMs to retrieve and reason across diverse modalities, (ii) facilitate downstream tasks as graph link prediction or molecular property prediction. 

* **Strengths:**
    * Rigorous dataset construction methodology, described in detail.
    * Clear articulation of the task definition and evaluation metrics.
    * Demonstrates the limitations of existing LLMs on this complex task.
    * Provides a valuable benchmark for future research in multi-modal RAG.
    * Clear integration of modalities to generate complex questions.
    * Synthetic data generation pipeline enhances knowledge graphs for high domain-expertise tasks.
    * Dataset is stratified and made available for training, validation, and testing.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The reliance on a single source (Wikipedia) for text background data may limit the diversity of the information available. Although the authors post-process Wikipedia data to make it less easily available and more complex, it may still limit generalization capability.
    * The relatively small size of the knowledge graph compared to those commonly used for GNN training (as the authors themselves acknowledge) restricts the performance of graph-based retrieval methods.
    * The text processing stage only considers LLMs to re-write drug background data due to protein data having an average token size that is lower than the threshold.

* **Potential Influence:** The dataset has the potential to significantly influence research in multi-modal RAG, particularly in the healthcare and bioinformatics domains. It should encourage the development of more sophisticated retrieval and reasoning methods that can effectively integrate diverse knowledge sources.

**Justification:**
BioMol-MQA is a well-constructed and timely dataset that addresses a clear need for more challenging benchmarks in the field of LLMs and RAG. While the authors were judicious in the design decisions made with respect to the size and scope of the project, the dataset's scope is somewhat limited by its narrow focus on polypharmacy. Despite this drawback, the combination of question-answering alongside domain-specific multimodal information presents a unique benchmark that should greatly stimulate research.

- **Score**: 8/10

### dots.llm1 Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05767v1)
- **Authors**: Bi Huo, Bin Tu, Cheng Qin, Da Zheng, Debing Zhang, Dongjie Zhang, En Li, Fu Guo, Jian Yao, Jie Lou, Junfeng Tian, Li Hu, Ran Zhu, Shengdong Chen, Shuo Liu, Su Guang, Te Wo, Weijun Zhang, Xiaoming Shi, Xinxin Peng, Xing Wu, Yawen Liu, Yuqiu Ji, Ze Wen, Zhenhai Liu, Zichao Li, Zilong Liao
- **Abstract**: Mixture of Experts (MoE) models have emerged as a promising paradigm for scaling language models efficiently by activating only a subset of parameters for each input token. In this report, we present dots.llm1, a large-scale MoE model that activates 14B parameters out of a total of 142B parameters, delivering performance on par with state-of-the-art models while reducing training and inference costs. Leveraging our meticulously crafted and efficient data processing pipeline, dots.llm1 achieves performance comparable to Qwen2.5-72B after pretraining on 11.2T high-quality tokens and post-training to fully unlock its capabilities. Notably, no synthetic data is used during pretraining. To foster further research, we open-source intermediate training checkpoints at every one trillion tokens, providing valuable insights into the learning dynamics of large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided technical report:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces `dots.11m1`, a 142B parameter Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language model, where only 14B parameters are active during inference.  The authors highlight that `dots.11m1` achieves competitive performance compared to open-source models like Qwen2.5-72B while using significantly fewer active parameters. The paper details the model architecture, a three-stage data processing pipeline, infrastructure optimizations like interleaved 1F1B-based communication and computation overlap, and efficient grouped GEMM implementation.  Key claims are centered on cost efficiency, performance, and open accessibility, including releasing intermediate training checkpoints to encourage further research.  Evaluations are performed across various English and Chinese language understanding, mathematics, and coding benchmarks, and an analysis of expert specialization within the MoE architecture is provided.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Contribution:** The paper presents a tangible, working model (`dots.11m1`) that could be valuable to researchers and practitioners seeking a cost-effective alternative to larger dense models. The focus on inference efficiency is particularly relevant in the current landscape of large language models.
*   **Detailed Implementation:**  The paper provides details on the data processing pipeline and infrastructure optimizations.  This is crucial for reproducibility and allows others to build upon their work. The detailed description of the training framework and infrastructure, including optimizations for MoE architectures, are helpful for others working on similar projects.
*   **Emphasis on Data Quality:**  The description of their three-stage data processing framework and the novel "Web Clutter Removal Model" suggests a strong focus on data quality which is a key factor in performance of LLMs.
*   **Open Accessibility:** The commitment to open-sourcing intermediate checkpoints is a significant contribution, enabling exploration of model learning dynamics.
*   **Strong Performance:** The models achieve performance on par with leading models while using fewer active parameters.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Novelty in Architecture:** The paper relies on existing architectures (DeepSeekMoE, MHA, QK-Norm), with relatively minor modifications. The architectural novelty appears limited.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While performance comparisons are provided, the paper lacks extensive ablation studies to isolate the impact of individual components (e.g., the auxiliary-loss-free strategy, specific data processing steps, or infrastructure optimizations). This makes it harder to understand the specific contributions of each design choice.
*   **Comparison Baselines:** The baselines are open-source alternatives, which are good, but it would be useful to have an analysis against closed-source models of similar size.
*   **Limited Long Context Evaluation:** While a context extension strategy is mentioned, the performance on the longer context RULER benchmark is somewhat disappointing (performing worse than Qwen2.5 72B).

**Significance:**

The paper's primary significance lies in demonstrating a practical approach to achieving competitive performance with a smaller "active" model size using MoE. This has implications for reducing inference costs and making large language models more accessible. The open accessibility of checkpoints is also valuable for the research community. The paper's focus on a good data processing pipeline is also notable.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid engineering effort that builds upon existing technologies to deliver a practical outcome: a reasonably performant and efficient MoE model. However, the limited architectural novelty and lack of in-depth ablation studies limit its overall impact. The results are mostly incremental improvements over existing approaches, rather than a revolutionary breakthrough.  The model's performance is solid, but not significantly superior to its comparators. The emphasis on opening the data and intermediate checkpoint makes this a valuable research asset.
