They benchmarked various LLMs (including Romanian-adapted models) on this dataset, revealing a performance gap: models performed better on international questions than on Romanian-specific ones. Further investigation involved machine translation of Romanian questions into English and cross-lingual tests using a French dataset. The findings highlight the influence of cultural context and data source on LLM performance, offering insights for creating culturally-aware multilingual NLP systems. The dataset is made publicly available on Hugging Face.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the *method of dataset creation* and the *focus on cultural context within a lower-resource language*. Deriving an NLP dataset from video recordings of a quiz show, rather than standard text corpora, is a fresh approach, and the detailed annotation scheme (cultural relevance, difficulty, topic) enhances its value. While other works address multilingualism and cultural biases in LLMs, the specific focus on Romanian, a mid-resource language, and the use of a unique data source are differentiating factors. Other than that, the analysis and evaluation process is standard.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from highlighting the performance disparities of LLMs across cultural contexts. The paper provides quantitative evidence that even state-of-the-art models struggle with culturally specific questions, even after being fine-tuned on Romanian data. This finding reinforces the need for more culturally sensitive and inclusive NLP systems. The open-sourcing of the WWTBM dataset provides a valuable resource for researchers working on multilingual and culturally-aware NLP, particularly those focusing on Romanian or similar mid-resource languages.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Innovative dataset creation methodology.
    *   Clear demonstration of cultural biases in LLM performance.
    *   Public availability of the dataset promotes further research.
    *   Thorough evaluation across different models and testing conditions (cultural context, difficulty, topic).
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset size is relatively small (around 1000 questions), which might limit the statistical power of some analyses.
    *   The dataset reflects the specific biases of the quiz show format, which might not fully represent broader Romanian culture.
    *   The evaluation is limited to multiple-choice questions.
    *   The performance metric is just accuracy, making analysis limited.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper is a valuable contribution, but not groundbreaking. It does a good job creating a new resource and demonstrating a point already shown by other papers in different languages. The novelty comes from the dataset creation and language.
    *   *Low*: The paper repeats known results in a different language.
    *   *High*: The paper contributes a large new resource to a language that is low-resourced in NLP.

Therefore, the paper is above average and has a significant impact on the romanian community.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Token Signature: Predicting Chain-of-Thought Gains with Token Decoding Feature in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06008v1)
- **Authors**: Peijie Liu, Fengli Xu, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) technique has proven effective in improving the performance of large language models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks. However, the performance gains are inconsistent across different tasks, and the underlying mechanism remains a long-standing research question. In this work, we make a preliminary observation that the monotonicity of token probability distributions may be correlated with the gains achieved through CoT reasoning. Leveraging this insight, we propose two indicators based on the token probability distribution to assess CoT effectiveness across different tasks. By combining instance-level indicators with logistic regression model, we introduce Dynamic CoT, a method that dynamically select between CoT and direct answer. Furthermore, we extend Dynamic CoT to closed-source models by transferring decision strategies learned from open-source models. Our indicators for assessing CoT effectiveness achieve an accuracy of 89.2\%, and Dynamic CoT reduces token consumption by more than 35\% while maintaining high accuracy. Overall, our work offers a novel perspective on the underlying mechanisms of CoT reasoning and provides a framework for its more efficient deployment.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and significance score for the paper "Token Signature: Predicting Chain-of-Thought Gains with Token Decoding Feature in Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel approach to understanding and predicting the effectiveness of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting in Large Language Models (LLMs).  The core idea revolves around analyzing the token probability distributions generated during the decoding process.  The authors hypothesize that the "monotonicity" (trend) of these distributions correlates with the gains achieved through CoT. They propose two indicators, Instance Spearman Correlation (Instance SC) and Aggregated Spearman Correlation (Aggregated SC), based on Spearman's correlation to capture these trends.  These indicators are then used in a logistic regression model to create "Dynamic CoT," a method that dynamically selects between CoT and direct answer (DA) prompting at the instance level.  Finally, they address the challenge of applying this method to closed-source models by transferring the decision strategy learned from open-source models using a voting mechanism.  The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of their approach across various benchmarks, showing improved accuracy and reduced token consumption compared to standard CoT prompting.

**Critical Evaluation and Significance:**

The paper presents an interesting and potentially valuable contribution to the field. Here's a detailed breakdown:

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty of the Approach:** The idea of using token probability distributions to predict CoT gains is innovative.  Most previous work has focused on task categories or high-level thematic trends. Looking at the decoding process itself provides a more fine-grained perspective. The concept of "Token Signature" is a good way to encapsulate this idea.
    *   **Clear Hypothesis and Methodology:** The hypothesis that the token probability distribution reflects model confidence and is correlated with CoT gain is clearly stated and well-motivated. The methodology is sound, and the two proposed indicators seem reasonable for capturing the desired trends. The use of Spearman's correlation is justified.
    *   **Dynamic CoT for Efficiency:**  The Dynamic CoT method is a practical application of their findings, offering a way to improve the efficiency of LLMs by reducing unnecessary token generation. The idea of dynamically choosing between CoT and direct answer is not entirely new, but this work provides a specific mechanism based on token probabilities.
    *   **Addressing Closed-Source Models:** The transfer learning approach for closed-source models is a significant contribution, as it makes their method more widely applicable. The voting strategy, while simple, provides a viable solution.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes extensive experimental results across a range of benchmarks and models.  The results generally support their claims, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed indicators and the Dynamic CoT method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While the paper provides an "intuitive theoretical analysis," a more rigorous theoretical framework for understanding the connection between token probability distributions and CoT gains would strengthen the work. Why *specifically* does monotonicity matter? What are the deeper connections between the token signature and the underlying mechanisms of CoT reasoning? A theoretical explanation could give more confidence in the approach's generality.
    *   **Simplicity of the Voting Mechanism:** The voting mechanism for closed-source models, while practical, is relatively simple. More sophisticated ensemble methods could potentially yield better results. Furthermore, there's a lack of detailed justification for the choice of voting. Are there other ensemble techniques that might be a better fit? How are the weights of the ensembled models determined?
    *   **Dependency on Greedy Decoding:**  The analysis relies on greedy decoding to obtain the token probability distributions. It's not clear how the approach would generalize to more complex decoding strategies like beam search or sampling-based methods, and whether the Token Signature would be as useful.
    *   **Dependence of the length of the token** The results show that using the first 50 tokens yields better classification results. What if the optimal number of tokens is different based on the model type and the given question?

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper sheds light on the internal workings of LLMs during CoT reasoning, offering a new perspective based on token probability distributions.
    *   The Dynamic CoT method has the potential to improve the efficiency of LLMs, making them more practical for real-world applications.
    *   The transfer learning approach enables the use of this method with closed-source models, expanding its impact.
    *   The work provides a foundation for further research into understanding and controlling the reasoning processes of LLMs.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with a novel approach, clear methodology, and promising empirical results. However, the lack of a more in-depth theoretical explanation and the simplicity of the transfer learning mechanism limit its impact somewhat.

**Score: 7.0**

**Justification:**

The paper scores a 7.0 because it is a well-executed study with a novel idea and useful practical applications. The strong empirical results demonstrate the potential of the approach. The main drawbacks are the limited theoretical depth and the relatively simple transfer learning method, which prevents the paper from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. This does not mean that the approach is not without value. This approach shows that the inner workings of LLMs is not a black box, and there are some indications or signals that we can use to predict the overall effectiveness of the model. A higher score would require a deeper theoretical framework and more exploration of alternative transfer learning strategies.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unlocking Recursive Thinking of LLMs: Alignment via Refinement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06009v1)
- **Authors**: Haoke Zhang, Xiaobo Liang, Cunxiang Wang, Juntao Li, Min Zhang
- **Abstract**: The OpenAI o1-series models have demonstrated that leveraging long-form Chain of Thought (CoT) can substantially enhance performance. However, the recursive thinking capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) remain limited, particularly in the absence of expert-curated data for distillation. In this paper, we propose \textbf{AvR}: \textbf{Alignment via Refinement}, a novel method aimed at unlocking the potential of LLMs for recursive reasoning through long-form CoT. AvR introduces a refinement process that integrates criticism and improvement actions, guided by differentiable learning techniques to optimize \textbf{refinement-aware rewards}. As a result, the synthesized multi-round data can be organized as a long refinement thought, further enabling test-time scaling. Experimental results show that AvR significantly outperforms conventional preference optimization methods. Notably, with only 3k synthetic samples, our method boosts the performance of the LLaMA-3-8B-Instruct model by over 20\% in win rate on AlpacaEval 2.0. Our code is available at Github (https://github.com/Banner-Z/AvR.git).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Unlocking Recursive Thinking of LLMs: Alignment via Refinement":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Alignment via Refinement" (AvR), a novel method designed to enhance the recursive thinking capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) using long-form Chain of Thought (CoT). AvR employs a refinement process that integrates criticism and improvement actions, guided by differentiable learning to optimize refinement-aware rewards. This results in multi-round data that forms a long refinement thought, which can then be scaled during inference. The method is demonstrated to outperform conventional preference optimization, significantly boosting performance (over 20% win rate on AlpacaEval 2.0 with the LLaMA-3-8B-Instruct model, using only 3k synthetic samples). The core idea is to shift from optimizing based solely on final outputs to rewarding the refinement process itself. The paper proposes a two-stage framework: first, a model is trained to refine responses through criticism and improvement; second, this refined model is used to generate high-quality CoT data to further guide the model to produce long-term refinement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its refinement-aware reward mechanism. Traditional LLM alignment focuses on optimizing final outputs, while AvR explicitly incentivizes the iterative process of reflection and improvement. The two-stage framework to both synthesize refined data and align the LLM with this process is another novel aspect. The explicit connection to differential learning (Sutton, 1988) and its application in this context are potentially significant. However, the individual components, such as using DPO or RSFT, are not novel. The combination and application within a recursive reasoning framework are what contribute to its novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the potential to unlock more sophisticated reasoning abilities in LLMs with less computational overhead compared to existing methods like online RL or extensive expert data. The fact that substantial gains are shown with only 3k synthetic samples is a compelling indication of the method's efficiency. The experimental results are convincing in demonstrating the performance gains on AlpacaEval 2.0 and Arena-Hard v0.1, and the analysis of iteration capabilities provides insights into how AvR improves self-improvement.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper effectively identifies the limitations of current LLMs in recursive reasoning and addresses the need for more efficient alignment methods.
    *   **Well-defined method:** The AvR approach is clearly described, with sufficient detail on the refinement process, reward mechanism, and two-stage framework.
    *   **Solid experimental results:** The experimental section demonstrates the effectiveness of AvR on established benchmarks, comparing it to relevant baselines. The analysis of different training data sizes and iterations provides valuable insights.
    *   **Addresses a key challenge:** The research tackles the challenge of achieving effective test-time scaling, a current research priority in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited novelty of individual components:** While the overall approach is novel, the individual techniques employed (DPO, RSFT) are well-established. The contribution relies on the specific combination of these techniques and the way they are applied.
    *   **Dependence on Reward Model:** The performance of AvR heavily relies on the quality and characteristics of the reward model (Skywork-Reward-Gemma-2-27B-v0.2). While the paper mentions this, a deeper investigation into the robustness and sensitivity of AvR to different reward models would strengthen the work.
    *   **Limited Investigation of Failure Modes:** The paper mostly focuses on demonstrating success. An exploration of cases where AvR fails or produces suboptimal refinements would provide a more complete understanding of its limitations.
    *   **Clarity regarding prompts** The appendix mentions used prompts but only in the context of model training and inference and with limited explanations. More examples showcasing prompts would be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work could influence future research by encouraging the development of refinement-aware alignment methods, potentially leading to more efficient and powerful LLMs.

*   **Justification:** The assigned score is based on the novelty of the combination of techniques, the clear gains demonstrated experimentally, and the potential for impact in the field. While the individual components are not groundbreaking, the framework proposed in the paper provides a novel and effective way to tackle a significant problem in LLM research. The weaknesses regarding the reliance on the reward model and the need for more comprehensive failure mode analysis somewhat temper the score. The method has limited influence on the work and does not provide deeper insight on edge cases.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### On the Merits of LLM-Based Corpus Enrichment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06015v1)
- **Authors**: Gal Zur, Tommy Mordo, Moshe Tennenholtz, Oren Kurland
- **Abstract**: Generative AI (genAI) technologies -- specifically, large language models (LLMs) -- and search have evolving relations. We argue for a novel perspective: using genAI to enrich a document corpus so as to improve query-based retrieval effectiveness. The enrichment is based on modifying existing documents or generating new ones. As an empirical proof of concept, we use LLMs to generate documents relevant to a topic which are more retrievable than existing ones. In addition, we demonstrate the potential merits of using corpus enrichment for retrieval augmented generation (RAG) and answer attribution in question answering.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper explores the idea of using Large Language Models (LLMs) to *enrich* a document corpus in order to improve query-based retrieval effectiveness. This enrichment involves either modifying existing documents or generating new ones, with the goal of making them more retrievable. The authors present an empirical study to support this idea, specifically demonstrating the potential benefits for:
1.  Standard ad hoc retrieval.
2.  Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) in question answering.
3.  Answer attribution in question answering.

The core concept is that LLMs can be used offline to address challenges like vocabulary mismatch, query variance, and fairness in document exposure, ultimately leading to better search results and downstream QA performance. They present novel metrics of measuring the faithfulness of newly generated content to that of the source corpus, and outline approaches for document modification, and document summary generation as ways to improve retrieval effectiveness and reduce hallucinations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs for *corpus enrichment* to improve retrieval, rather than just query reformulation or ranking, is reasonably novel. While LLMs have been used for various aspects of search, this specific offline, corpus-focused approach is a distinct angle. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental. Related work exists on document expansion, and while LLMs offer a new tool for expansion, the fundamental concept is not entirely new. The idea of using LLMs to promote fairness in information exposure by modifying documents so they are more retrievable is also novel but it may not be the best approach to solving this issue. The metrics on faithfulness are a solid contribution to the space, considering hallucinations have been a prominent issue in RAG and QA.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance is moderate to high. If this approach proves effective and scalable, it could have a tangible impact on search engine design and QA systems. By pre-processing the corpus, it could alleviate some of the real-time computational burden of LLM-based search and QA. The demonstration of improved RAG and attribution performance is also significant, as these are crucial aspects of modern QA systems. The potential of this work to address exposure fairness and vocabulary mismatch is also a valuable consideration.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges in query-based retrieval and how LLMs can be leveraged to address them from a corpus-centric perspective.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes an empirical study to support its claims, providing concrete evidence of the potential benefits of corpus enrichment.
    *   **Focus on Faithfulness:** Addresses the critical issue of hallucination.
    *   **Multiple Applications:** Demonstrates the approach's relevance to ad hoc retrieval, RAG, and attribution, showcasing its versatility.
    *   **Exploration of different document enrichment methods.** Exploration of zero-shot and context-aware methods provides empirical evidence for their performance in relevant retrieval settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generality:** The experimental setup is relatively specific (using particular datasets, LLMs, and ranking functions). The results may not generalize to other scenarios. The performance of the document modification might not be sustainable in the long run due to the iterative improvements in search algorithms, potentially decreasing their ranking over time.
    *   **Oracle Document Selection:** In the RAG experiments, the use of an oracle for document selection is a significant limitation. It doesn't reflect a real-world scenario where relevant documents are not known *a priori*. The experiments for RAG would have benefited by using a traditional method for document selection, like BM25.
    *   **Simple Generation Methods:** The generation methods are quite basic (primarily query-biased summarization/rephrasing). More sophisticated generation techniques might yield even better results. The selection of GPT-4 and Llama3 may restrict findings to only specific LLMs. The experiment could have benefited from broader exploration of LLMs to compare the quality of documents created for enrichment.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:**  The paper doesn't thoroughly address the scalability of corpus enrichment for large, dynamic corpora like the Web.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Stronger Baselines:** The ad hoc retrieval experiments do not include comparisons to state-of-the-art retrieval models that are explicitly trained for retrieval, which could better contextualize the improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence research in several directions:
    *   Encouraging the development of more sophisticated LLM-based corpus enrichment techniques.
    *   Motivating further investigation into the trade-offs between corpus pre-processing and real-time query processing.
    *   Inspiring new approaches to addressing fairness and bias in search engine results.

**Score:**

Taking all of the above into consideration, I would give this paper a **Score: 6**. The core idea is reasonably novel and has the potential to be significant, but the experimental limitations (especially the oracle document selection in the RAG experiments) and the basic generation methods limit the strength of the conclusions and the overall impact. The lack of comparison to stronger, state-of-the-art retrieval models further contributes to this score. While the contributions around faithfulness metrics and a focus on corpus-centric perspective are valuable, the paper requires further work to demonstrate its robustness and scalability.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Optimization-Free Universal Watermark Forgery with Regenerative Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06018v1)
- **Authors**: Chaoyi Zhu, Zaitang Li, Renyi Yang, Robert Birke, Pin-Yu Chen, Tsung-Yi Ho, Lydia Y. Chen
- **Abstract**: Watermarking becomes one of the pivotal solutions to trace and verify the origin of synthetic images generated by artificial intelligence models, but it is not free of risks. Recent studies demonstrate the capability to forge watermarks from a target image onto cover images via adversarial optimization without knowledge of the target generative model and watermark schemes. In this paper, we uncover a greater risk of an optimization-free and universal watermark forgery that harnesses existing regenerative diffusion models. Our proposed forgery attack, PnP (Plug-and-Plant), seamlessly extracts and integrates the target watermark via regenerating the image, without needing any additional optimization routine. It allows for universal watermark forgery that works independently of the target image's origin or the watermarking model used. We explore the watermarked latent extracted from the target image and visual-textual context of cover images as priors to guide sampling of the regenerative process. Extensive evaluation on 24 scenarios of model-data-watermark combinations demonstrates that PnP can successfully forge the watermark (up to 100% detectability and user attribution), and maintain the best visual perception. By bypassing model retraining and enabling adaptability to any image, our approach significantly broadens the scope of forgery attacks, presenting a greater challenge to the security of current watermarking techniques for diffusion models and the authority of watermarking schemes in synthetic data generation and governance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PnP (Plug-and-Plant), a novel, optimization-free, and universal watermark forgery attack targeting generative diffusion models. The attack extracts the watermark from a target image using a proxy diffusion model and then integrates it into a cover image through a regenerative diffusion model.  PnP leverages the latent space structure and visual-textual guidance to control the regenerative process, allowing for watermark forgery that's independent of the original model or watermarking scheme.  Experiments across various model-data-watermark combinations demonstrate the attack's effectiveness, achieving high watermark detectability and user attribution while maintaining visual quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The most novel aspect is the **optimization-free** nature of the attack.  Prior forgery methods required computationally intensive adversarial optimization (e.g., Imprint).  PnP bypasses this by leveraging pre-trained regenerative diffusion models, making forgery far more efficient.  The "plug-and-play" aspect, where the attacker can simply use an existing regenerative model as a forgery tool, is also noteworthy. The idea of harnessing regenerative models (super-resolution, face restoration) *specifically for watermark forgery* appears to be original. However, it builds upon prior work in watermark extraction from diffusion models, so the core concept isn't entirely new. The use of visual-textual guidance to condition the regenerative process is also a standard technique, though its application to watermark forgery is important.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in **exposing a critical vulnerability** in current watermarking schemes for diffusion models. By demonstrating that watermarks can be readily forged without model retraining or specialized expertise, the paper challenges the reliance on existing watermarking techniques for data governance and provenance tracking. This highlights the **urgent need** for more robust and forgery-resistant watermarking methods. The improved efficiency compared to existing method Imprint, makes the method more realistic for large-scale attacks. The paper effectively shows how easily an adversary with limited resources can undermine watermarking schemes.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Optimization-free and universal:**  A major practical advantage.
    *   **Good empirical validation:**  The paper presents thorough experiments across a diverse set of target models, datasets, and watermarking techniques, strengthening the findings. The inclusion of multiple evaluation metrics (detectability, visual quality) provides a well-rounded assessment.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The method and experimental setup are explained clearly, and the paper includes visualizations that aid understanding.
    *   **Discussion of limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations related to performance variations across models and the need to tune the method for trade-offs between forgery success and image quality.
    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** Digital watermarking forgery and provenance are critical concerns.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Proxy Model:** Like previous attacks, this method relies on a proxy diffusion model for latent estimation. While the authors argue this is a realistic assumption, the quality of the latent extraction (and therefore, the forgery) is dependent on how close the proxy model is to the true target model. Further analysis of the impact of proxy model similarity would be useful.
    *   **Performance Variations:** The method doesn't work uniformly well across all diffusion models. The authors acknowledge the varying architectures of target models affect results, which creates difficulty in generalizing the technique.
    *   **Tradeoffs:** The performance varies on different datasets and models. The authors did not address whether there exists a single framework to adjust across different settings.
    *   **Limited Novelty beyond optimization-free component.** Many components such as image conditioning and text prompting is inherited from other image generation or edit works.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence research in the watermarking and data governance communities. It motivates the development of more robust, forgery-resistant watermarking techniques and highlights the importance of considering realistic attack scenarios.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novelty of the optimization-free framework, the significance of the demonstrated vulnerability, and the comprehensive empirical evaluation, the paper warrants a strong score. However, the reliance on existing techniques for conditioning and latent space manipulation, combined with the performance variations across different models, keeps it from being truly groundbreaking. Also, the watermarks forged still come with quality defects. Despite these limitations, its real-world impact is substantial.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.MM
- **Score**: 7/10

### When to Trust Context: Self-Reflective Debates for Context Reliability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06020v1)
- **Authors**: Zeqi Zhou, Fang Wu, Shayan Talaei, Haokai Zhao, Cheng Meixin, Tinson Xu, Amin Saberi, Yejin Choi
- **Abstract**: Large language models frequently encounter conflicts between their parametric knowledge and contextual input, often resulting in factual inconsistencies or hallucinations. We propose Self-Reflective Debate for Contextual Reliability (SR-DCR), a lightweight framework that integrates token-level self-confidence with an asymmetric multi-agent debate to adjudicate such conflicts. A critic, deprived of context, challenges a defender who argues from the given passage; a judge model evaluates the debate and determines the context's reliability. The final answer is selected by combining the verdict with model confidence. Experiments on the ClashEval benchmark demonstrate that SR-DCR consistently enhances robustness to misleading context while maintaining accuracy on trustworthy inputs, outperforming both classical debate and confidence-only baselines with minimal computational overhead. The code is available at https://github.com/smiles724/Self-Reflective-Debates.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Self-Reflective Debate for Contextual Reliability (SR-DCR), a novel framework designed to help large language models (LLMs) reconcile conflicting information from their internal parametric knowledge and external contextual input. SR-DCR combines token-level self-confidence with an asymmetric multi-agent debate. In this debate, a context-deprived "critic" challenges a "defender" who argues based on the provided context. A "judge" model evaluates the debate and determines the context's reliability. The final answer is then selected by considering both the context reliability judgment and the model's confidence in its prior knowledge. Experiments on the ClashEval benchmark demonstrate that SR-DCR enhances robustness to misleading context while preserving accuracy on reliable inputs, outperforming standard debate and confidence-only methods with minimal overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific combination of existing techniques in a way tailored to address the internal vs. external knowledge conflict. While multi-agent debate (MAD) and self-confidence scores have been explored separately, the asymmetric debate design, where one agent *lacks* the context, is a notable contribution. The integration of token-level confidence into the decision-making process adds another layer of nuance.

**Significance:** The problem of LLMs hallucinating or generating factually inconsistent outputs due to conflicting knowledge sources is a significant one, especially in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems.  SR-DCR offers a practical approach to mitigating this issue, and the demonstrated improvements on ClashEval suggest that it is a promising direction.  The paper’s systematic analysis of how different levels of contextual perturbation affect model behavior is also a valuable contribution to understanding LLM failure modes.  The findings on the tension between debate rounds and context reliance are insightful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of internal-external knowledge conflict in LLMs.
*   **Well-Defined Framework:** SR-DCR is clearly explained, with well-defined roles for the critic, defender, and judge agents.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on ClashEval provide convincing evidence of SR-DCR's effectiveness. The ablation studies are particularly useful for understanding the contribution of each component.
*   **Systematic Analysis:** The graded contextual perturbations and knowledge categorization provide a rich understanding of LLM behavior.
*   **Practical Implementation:** The framework has a reasonable computational overhead which promotes use in practical contexts

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Existing Techniques:** While the combination is novel, the individual components (MAD, confidence scores) are well-established. Therefore its novelty is derived from the *integration*, as opposed to the creation of fundamentally new methods.
*   **Benchmark Limitations:** While ClashEval is a suitable benchmark, it's a synthetic dataset.  Generalizability to more diverse and complex real-world scenarios needs further exploration.
*   **Fixed Thresholds:**  The use of fixed confidence thresholds might limit the framework's adaptability across different domains and tasks. This could be partially addressed via adaptive thresholding methods.
*   **Limited Scope of Exploration:** The paper focuses primarily on factual knowledge conflicts.  It would be interesting to see how SR-DCR handles conflicts related to reasoning, opinions, or biases.

**Potential Influence:** The SR-DCR framework has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

*   **RAG Systems:** The method can be easily integrated into RAG systems to improve the reliability of generated content.
*   **Multi-Agent Systems:**  The asymmetric debate design could inspire new MAD frameworks that better address informational imbalances.
*   **Factuality and Hallucination Research:** The paper provides a valuable approach to studying and mitigating factual errors in LLMs.
*   **Knowledge Conflict Resolution:**  The insights into how LLMs resolve knowledge conflicts can inform the development of more robust and trustworthy models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by intelligently combining existing techniques to tackle a significant challenge in LLMs. The asymmetric debate design and self-confidence integration demonstrate clear thinking and offer a practical approach to improving factual reliability. However, the reliance on existing techniques and limitations in dataset variety preclude a higher rating.  Therefore:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Restereo: Diffusion stereo video generation and restoration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06023v1)
- **Authors**: Xingchang Huang, Ashish Kumar Singh, Florian Dubost, Cristina Nader Vasconcelos, Sakar Khattar, Liang Shi, Christian Theobalt, Cengiz Oztireli, Gurprit Singh
- **Abstract**: Stereo video generation has been gaining increasing attention with recent advancements in video diffusion models. However, most existing methods focus on generating 3D stereoscopic videos from monocular 2D videos. These approaches typically assume that the input monocular video is of high quality, making the task primarily about inpainting occluded regions in the warped video while preserving disoccluded areas. In this paper, we introduce a new pipeline that not only generates stereo videos but also enhances both left-view and right-view videos consistently with a single model. Our approach achieves this by fine-tuning the model on degraded data for restoration, as well as conditioning the model on warped masks for consistent stereo generation. As a result, our method can be fine-tuned on a relatively small synthetic stereo video datasets and applied to low-quality real-world videos, performing both stereo video generation and restoration. Experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms existing approaches both qualitatively and quantitatively in stereo video generation from low-resolution inputs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Restereo: Diffusion stereo video generation and restoration":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel pipeline for simultaneously generating and restoring stereo videos using a single diffusion model. Unlike existing methods that primarily focus on generating stereo videos from high-quality monocular videos (mainly inpainting the occluded regions during warping), this work tackles the challenge of generating stereo videos from potentially low-quality input videos. The key idea is to fine-tune a pre-trained video diffusion model by incorporating data degradation (noise, blur, compression) during training and conditioning the model on warped masks for consistent stereo generation. This approach allows the model to not only generate a consistent right-view video but also enhance the left-view video quality.  Experiments on synthetic datasets demonstrate the method's superiority over existing techniques, particularly when dealing with low-resolution inputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the simultaneous stereo video generation and restoration using a single diffusion model fine-tuned with degraded data. The idea of incorporating data degradation during training, inspired by Real-ESRGAN, is a well-established technique for image/video restoration. However, applying it specifically within a stereo video generation framework and combining it with warped mask conditioning represents a unique and valuable contribution. Fine-tuning a *single* model for *simultaneous* stereo generation *and* restoration is also a key differentiator. Existing methods typically either focus on generating the stereo view, or restoring/super-resolutioning the view. This is especially relevant because stereo image training data is not usually available to train from scratch.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical limitation of existing stereo video generation methods, which often assume high-quality input. By handling low-resolution inputs, the proposed method broadens the applicability of stereo video generation in real-world scenarios. The use of synthetic data for training, coupled with data augmentation strategies, makes the approach more accessible than methods reliant on large-scale, real-world stereo video datasets. Quantitative and qualitative results seem to support the claims, but the user study is limited in scope.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a practical problem:** Generating stereo videos from potentially degraded real-world inputs.
    *   **Single model for both generation and restoration:** This simplifies the pipeline and potentially enforces consistency between the views.
    *   **Data augmentation strategy:**  Leveraging degraded data during training enhances robustness and enables restoration.
    *   **Use of synthetic data:** Reduces the dependency on scarce real-world stereo video datasets.
    *   **Clear experimental setup:**  Well-defined evaluation metrics and comparisons to relevant baselines.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic data dependency:** Although the use of synthetic data is a strength, it may limit the model's ability to generalize to all real-world scenarios. The diversity of synthetic objects are necessarily limited compared to the real world.
    *   **Limited user study:**  A larger and more diverse user study would strengthen the perceptual evaluation.
    *   **Depth estimation reliance:** The inference pipeline relies on external depth estimators (DepthCrafter), which can introduce errors.  The paper acknowledges this and uses post-processing steps, but the performance is inherently tied to the quality of the depth estimation.
    *   **Lack of failure case analysis:** More analysis on the types of scenarios where the method struggles is warranted.  The paper mentions specular highlights but could delve deeper.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and practical stereo video generation systems. The idea of combining generation and restoration in a single model, using data augmentation to handle low-quality inputs, could be adopted in other related tasks. This should make stereo video creation far more accessible to consumers.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The core idea is a clever combination of existing techniques within a stereo video context.  The experiments are reasonably thorough given the scope of the task. The limitations are reasonably clearly delineated. The technical novelty, while building upon established techniques, is well-motivated and practically useful.

**Score: 7**

The paper presents a technically sound and practically relevant contribution to the field of stereo video generation. While it relies on existing techniques like data degradation, the combination of these techniques within a stereo video framework and the design of a single model for generation and restoration justifies a score of 7. More thorough testing with real-world videos would have pushed the score higher.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models are Demonstration Pre-Selectors for Themselves
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06033v1)
- **Authors**: Jiarui Jin, Yuwei Wu, Haoxuan Li, Xiaoting He, Weinan Zhang, Yiming Yang, Yong Yu, Jun Wang, Mengyue Yang
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) with large language models (LLMs) delivers strong few-shot performance by choosing few-shot demonstrations from the entire training data. However, existing ICL methods, which rely on similarity or diversity scores to choose demonstrations, incur high computational costs due to repeatedly retrieval from large-scale datasets for each query. To this end, we propose FEEDER (FEw yet Essential Demonstration prE-selectoR), a novel pre-selection framework that identifies a representative subset of demonstrations containing the most representative examples in the training data, tailored to specific LLMs. To construct this subset, we introduce the "sufficiency" and "necessity" metrics in the pre-selection stage and design a tree-based algorithm to identify representative examples efficiently. Once pre-selected, this representative subset can effectively replace the full training data, improving efficiency while maintaining comparable performance in ICL. Additionally, our pre-selected subset also benefits fine-tuning LLMs, where we introduce a bi-level optimization method that enhances training efficiency without sacrificing performance. Experiments with LLMs ranging from 300M to 8B parameters show that FEEDER can reduce training data size by over 20% while maintaining performance and seamlessly integrating with various downstream demonstration selection strategies in ICL.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces FEEDER, a novel pre-selection framework for demonstrations in in-context learning (ICL) and fine-tuning with Large Language Models (LLMs). FEEDER identifies a representative subset of the training data by introducing the metrics of "sufficiency" and "necessity," and uses a tree-based algorithm to efficiently identify representative examples tailored to specific LLMs. This pre-selected subset can replace the full training data, improving efficiency while maintaining performance in ICL and enhancing the fine-tuning process.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its LLM-aware approach to demonstration selection. Instead of relying solely on similarity or diversity scores, which are task-agnostic, FEEDER leverages the few-shot capabilities of LLMs to assess the value of demonstrations relative to the LLM's own knowledge and capabilities. The "sufficiency" and "necessity" metrics provide a new lens through which to view demonstration selection, addressing the redundancy and irrelevance problems that can arise with traditional methods. Additionally, FEEDER presents a bi-level optimization framework that simultaneously selects the representative subset and tunes the LLM.

The significance stems from the fact that in-context learning is heavily dependent on the demonstrations used, and fine-tuning remains computationally expensive. By reducing the training data size while maintaining (or even improving) performance, FEEDER addresses a key bottleneck in applying LLMs to various tasks. This can potentially lower computational costs and enable wider adoption of LLMs, especially in resource-constrained environments. The observation that this approach can also improve fine-tuning efficiency adds further practical value. The paper also includes an empirical analysis showing that a small LLM can be used for selecting the pre-training data for a larger LLM, further enhancing practical adoption and efficiency.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Metrics:** The introduction of "sufficiency" and "necessity" as LLM-aware metrics is a strong contribution.
*   **Practical Application:** The pre-selection framework and bi-level optimization approach offer clear practical benefits in terms of efficiency and performance.
*   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across multiple LLMs, datasets, and demonstration selection strategies demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of FEEDER.
*   **Ablation Studies:** Comprehensive ablation studies show that FEEDER can reduce training data size while often improving performance
*   **Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written, providing clear definitions, and thorough explanations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:**  While the paper aims to improve efficiency, the tree-based approximation algorithm itself can have O(log TRAIN) time complexity. Although the paper claims the algorithm can be iterated across multiple runs to further refine components, this will add to the computational cost. This computational trade-off must be carefully examined in real-world scenarios. The paper also claims varying R (i.e. # of runs) has minimal impact. Further analysis should be done as to why the minimal impact occurs, and a more exhaustive benchmark should be included.
*   **Bias Amplification:** The method relies on the LLM itself to evaluate demonstrations. If the LLM exhibits certain biases, FEEDER could potentially amplify them in the selected subset. While the authors acknowledge this in the impact statement, this potential issue needs to be more deeply investigated and mitigated. While, with a single run (R=1) the complexity is simplified to O(log TRAIN) this does not directly address the computational cost since in real world implementation, one will not know how to optimally fine tune R until evaluation, which will require more benchmark testing.
*   **Simplified Settings:** The experimental settings, especially the selection of the one-shot setting are somewhat simplified. While the paper provides a clear theoretical framework for how the model operates at the instance and set level, more experiments with higher settings of K or R should have been considered.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers valuable insights and a practical framework for improving demonstration selection. While the potential for bias amplification and computational trade-offs associated with more complex runs require further investigation, the novelty and potential impact on the field of ICL and fine-tuning justify a positive evaluation.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MATP-BENCH: Can MLLM Be a Good Automated Theorem Prover for Multimodal Problems?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06034v1)
- **Authors**: Zhitao He, Zongwei Lyu, Dazhong Chen, Dadi Guo, Yi R. Fung
- **Abstract**: Numerous theorems, such as those in geometry, are often presented in multimodal forms (e.g., diagrams). Humans benefit from visual reasoning in such settings, using diagrams to gain intuition and guide the proof process. Modern Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in solving a wide range of mathematical problems. However, the potential of MLLMs as Automated Theorem Provers (ATPs), specifically in the multimodal domain, remains underexplored. In this paper, we introduce the Multimodal Automated Theorem Proving benchmark (MATP-BENCH), a new Multimodal, Multi-level, and Multi-language benchmark designed to evaluate MLLMs in this role as multimodal automated theorem provers. MATP-BENCH consists of 1056 multimodal theorems drawn from high school, university, and competition-level mathematics. All these multimodal problems are accompanied by formalizations in Lean 4, Coq and Isabelle, thus making the benchmark compatible with a wide range of theorem-proving frameworks. MATP-BENCH requires models to integrate sophisticated visual understanding with mastery of a broad spectrum of mathematical knowledge and rigorous symbolic reasoning to generate formal proofs. We use MATP-BENCH to evaluate a variety of advanced multimodal language models. Existing methods can only solve a limited number of the MATP-BENCH problems, indicating that this benchmark poses an open challenge for research on automated theorem proving.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MATP-BENCH, a new multimodal benchmark for evaluating the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) as automated theorem provers (ATPs).  MATP-BENCH consists of 1056 theorems from high school, university, and competition-level mathematics, each accompanied by images and formalizations in Lean 4, Coq, and Isabelle.  The paper also presents results from evaluating various MLLMs on the benchmark, highlighting limitations in current models, especially in symbolic reasoning and proof construction within the formal language settings. The authors identify the primary bottleneck as the ability to utilize auxiliary constructions and perform complex logical reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the construction and release of the MATP-BENCH dataset. The multimodal aspect, multi-level difficulty, and multi-language formalizations are a significant step forward in ATP benchmarking. Datasets focused on ATP have traditionally been text-based, so adding a visual component is a significant contribution. While datasets exist that combine vision and mathematics, this dataset's focus on formal theorem proving is unique.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is twofold. First, it provides a valuable resource for researchers working on multimodal reasoning and automated theorem proving.  It addresses a gap in existing benchmarks by explicitly targeting the ability of MLLMs to integrate visual understanding with symbolic reasoning for formal proof generation.  Second, the experimental results reveal the current limitations of even state-of-the-art MLLMs in this challenging task. This highlights areas where future research should focus, specifically on improving symbolic reasoning capabilities, proper utilization of auxiliary constructions, and robust information understanding from both visual and language modalities. The error analysis provides insights for future research directions. The paper shows the limitations of end-to-end methods, highlighting a research opportunity to explore more guided or modular approaches.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dataset is well-motivated and addresses a clear need in the field.
    *   The dataset is diverse, spanning multiple difficulty levels and mathematical domains.
    *   The formalizations in multiple languages increase the utility of the dataset across a range of theorem proving frameworks.
    *   The experimental results are thorough and provide valuable insights into the limitations of existing models.
    *   The paper includes a detailed error analysis that helps point to future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental results mostly confirm existing knowledge that MLLMs struggle with formal reasoning.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on describing the dataset and evaluating existing models. It doesn't propose any novel methods for addressing the challenges it highlights.
    *   While the dataset is multi-language, the experiments are not exhaustive in each language.
    * The paper could be strengthened by a more in-depth exploration of specific error cases or examples of how MLLMs fail in particular types of proofs.

*   **Potential Impact:**  MATP-BENCH has the potential to significantly influence research in automated theorem proving and multimodal reasoning. It will likely serve as a standard benchmark for evaluating future MLLMs and drive progress in the development of more robust and reliable automated theorem provers. It also highlights potential areas for human-AI collaboration, particularly in the construction and verification of complex proofs.

*   **Critical Considerations:** It's essential to recognize that simply creating a benchmark, while valuable, doesn't automatically guarantee a high score. The significance of the benchmark lies in how it's used and whether it effectively challenges and advances the field. The paper clearly states the dataset’s capabilities, limitations, and design choices, indicating a comprehensive understanding of its contribution.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-motivated benchmark that addresses an important gap in the field of automated theorem proving. It provides a comprehensive evaluation of existing MLLMs and offers valuable insights for future research. The primary reason for the score not being higher is that the paper is largely descriptive and doesn't propose any novel methods. While the benchmark is a significant contribution, the lack of methodological innovation limits its overall impact. A score of 7 reflects the dataset's valuable contribution and potential influence, tempered by the absence of novel techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CP-Bench: Evaluating Large Language Models for Constraint Modelling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06052v1)
- **Authors**: Kostis Michailidis, Dimos Tsouros, Tias Guns
- **Abstract**: Combinatorial problems are present in a wide range of industries. Constraint Programming (CP) is a well-suited problem-solving paradigm, but its core process, namely constraint modelling, is a bottleneck for wider adoption. Aiming to alleviate this bottleneck, recent studies have explored using Large Language Models (LLMs) as modelling assistants, transforming combinatorial problem descriptions to executable constraint models, similar to coding assistants. However, the existing evaluation datasets for constraint modelling are often limited to small, homogeneous, or domain-specific instances, which do not capture the diversity of real-world scenarios. This work addresses this gap by introducing CP-Bench, a novel benchmark dataset that includes a diverse set of well-known combinatorial problem classes sourced from the CP community, structured explicitly for evaluating LLM-driven CP modelling. With this dataset, and given the variety of constraint modelling frameworks, we compare and evaluate the modelling capabilities of LLMs for three distinct constraint modelling systems, which vary in abstraction level and underlying syntax: the high-level MiniZinc language and Python-based CPMpy library, and the lower-level Python interface of the OR-Tools CP-SAT solver. In order to enhance the ability of LLMs to produce valid constraint models, we systematically evaluate the use of prompt-based and inference-time compute methods adapted from existing LLM-based code generation research. Our results underscore the modelling convenience provided by Python-based frameworks, as well as the effectiveness of documentation-rich system prompts, which, augmented with repeated sampling and self-verification, achieve further improvements, reaching up to 70\% accuracy on this new, highly challenging benchmark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CP-Bench: Evaluating Large Language Models for Constraint Modelling":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CP-Bench, a novel benchmark dataset designed specifically for evaluating the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the task of constraint modelling. The dataset comprises a diverse set of well-known combinatorial problem classes drawn from the constraint programming (CP) community.  The authors use CP-Bench to systematically evaluate the ability of state-of-the-art LLMs to generate executable constraint models in three distinct modelling systems: MiniZinc, CPMpy, and OR-Tools CP-SAT solver. The paper also explores various prompt-based and inference-time techniques, such as retrieval-augmented in-context learning (RAICL), repeated sampling with solution majority voting, and self-verification, to improve the accuracy of LLM-generated constraint models.  The results highlight the impact of framework abstraction level on LLM performance and demonstrate the effectiveness of documentation-rich prompts and inference-time compute methods in enhancing LLM-driven CP modelling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The introduction of CP-Bench is a significant contribution.  Existing benchmarks in this space tend to be limited in scope, either focused on specific domains or consisting of relatively simple instances. CP-Bench offers a much-needed diverse and challenging dataset that better reflects real-world combinatorial problems.  The systematic comparison of different constraint modeling frameworks (MiniZinc, CPMpy, OR-Tools) under a single, consistent evaluation framework is also novel. Applying techniques such as Retrieval-Augmented In-Context Learning, and self-verification for iteratively model refinement is also a contribution towards constraint solving.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in the wider adoption of constraint programming: the constraint modelling process itself. By investigating the potential of LLMs to automate or assist with this process, the paper opens up avenues for making CP more accessible to non-experts. The results shed light on the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in this domain, highlighting areas where they excel (e.g., Python-based frameworks, documentation-rich prompts) and areas where further research is needed (e.g., complex constraints, low-level interfaces). The comparison of the frameworks is also significant, as it gives insights into which are better suited for LLM driven automation, and more effort should be dedicated to them.
The experiments and analysis are thorough, and the paper is well-written and easy to follow. The results are clearly presented and discussed.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset:** CP-Bench fills a critical gap in the evaluation of LLMs for constraint modeling. The diversity and complexity of the dataset are crucial for assessing the practical applicability of LLM-driven CP modelling.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:**  The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of different modelling frameworks, prompt engineering strategies, and inference-time compute methods. This systematic approach allows for a better understanding of the factors that influence LLM performance in this task.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The research is directly relevant to the development of tools and techniques that can make constraint programming more accessible and user-friendly.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Solver Selection:** While the paper compares three modelling frameworks, the underlying constraint solver is not varied. It would be interesting to see how LLM-generated models perform with different solvers, especially for problem types where certain solvers are known to be more effective.
    *   **Scalability concerns:** The models and solvers used are more focused on teaching/convenience and less on solving very large problems. While the dataset has great diversity and represents well the constraint types, the impact of using the generated LLMs on very large problem domains is not explored, especially how efficiently they can generate the constraint types.
    *   **User Intervention:** The prompt engineering may require users to provide information that in some senses already describes a model. How easy is this for a user to generate who does not know constraint modeling already?
    *   **Solution Accuracy as Primary Metric:** relying solely on solution accuracy without considering modeling efficiency or the expressiveness of the generated models could be limiting. The paper could be strengthened by incorporating more nuanced metrics that capture these aspects.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to stimulate further research in the area of LLM-driven constraint modeling.  The CP-Bench dataset will serve as a valuable resource for the community, and the findings of the paper will inform the design of future systems and techniques. This work could also impact the broader field of automated problem-solving and decision-making by making combinatorial optimization techniques more widely available.

*The evaluation of the self-verification is not that deep, and it could be extended with other similar experiments.*

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a new, valuable dataset and systematically evaluating the performance of LLMs in constraint modelling.  The work provides insights into the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in this domain and opens up avenues for future research. However, the paper's score is somewhat tempered by the limited solver selection, reliance on solution accuracy as the sole evaluation metric, and scalability/efficiency concerns of the modeling. While valuable, the paper is a starting point, and future work will be needed to address these limitations and fully realize the potential of LLM-driven constraint modelling.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hey, That's My Data! Label-Only Dataset Inference in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06057v1)
- **Authors**: Chen Xiong, Zihao Wang, Rui Zhu, Tsung-Yi Ho, Pin-Yu Chen, Jingwei Xiong, Haixu Tang, Lucila Ohno-Machado
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized Natural Language Processing by excelling at interpreting, reasoning about, and generating human language. However, their reliance on large-scale, often proprietary datasets poses a critical challenge: unauthorized usage of such data can lead to copyright infringement and significant financial harm. Existing dataset-inference methods typically depend on log probabilities to detect suspicious training material, yet many leading LLMs have begun withholding or obfuscating these signals. This reality underscores the pressing need for label-only approaches capable of identifying dataset membership without relying on internal model logits. We address this gap by introducing CatShift, a label-only dataset-inference framework that capitalizes on catastrophic forgetting: the tendency of an LLM to overwrite previously learned knowledge when exposed to new data. If a suspicious dataset was previously seen by the model, fine-tuning on a portion of it triggers a pronounced post-tuning shift in the model's outputs; conversely, truly novel data elicits more modest changes. By comparing the model's output shifts for a suspicious dataset against those for a known non-member validation set, we statistically determine whether the suspicious set is likely to have been part of the model's original training corpus. Extensive experiments on both open-source and API-based LLMs validate CatShift's effectiveness in logit-inaccessible settings, offering a robust and practical solution for safeguarding proprietary data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Hey, That's My Data! Label-Only Dataset Inference in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CatShift, a novel label-only dataset inference framework designed to detect unauthorized use of proprietary datasets in Large Language Models (LLMs).  CatShift leverages the phenomenon of catastrophic forgetting: if a suspicious dataset was part of the model's training data, fine-tuning the LLM on that dataset will cause a pronounced shift in the model's output distribution because the model is "re-learning" previously forgotten information. If the dataset is new, the output shift is more modest. By comparing the output shifts on a suspicious dataset against a known non-member validation set, the method statistically determines if the suspicious set was likely part of the original training corpus. Experiments on open-source and API-based LLMs demonstrate the effectiveness of CatShift in logit-inaccessible settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on *label-only* dataset inference.  Existing dataset inference methods heavily rely on access to log probabilities or confidence scores, which are increasingly being withheld by LLM providers. CatShift directly addresses this limitation by using top-1 predicted tokens only, making it much more practically applicable. The idea to leverage *catastrophic forgetting* as a signal for dataset membership in this context is a novel approach, as well. However, the use of catastrophic forgetting *in general* is not new, as it's a well-established concept in machine learning. Thus, the key novelty is in *how* they apply it. The comparison against a baseline that uses top-1 prediction is a solid validation of catastrophic forgetting and that it works in practice in label-only settings.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is substantial. As LLMs become increasingly prevalent, the unauthorized use of proprietary data for training raises serious copyright and financial concerns. The ability to detect such unauthorized use, *even without access to internal model states*, is extremely valuable. CatShift provides a robust and practical solution for safeguarding proprietary data in a real-world deployment scenario that other existing methods cannot address. This is particularly relevant considering that some LLMs like Anthropic Claude are already withholding log probabilities in the output. The extension to the API-based model evaluation with known members and non-members adds strength and indicates the approach may generalize more broadly.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a critical and growing problem: the unauthorized use of training data.
    *   Practical applicability: Works in label-only settings, making it usable with commercial LLMs.
    *   Novel approach: Effectively leverages catastrophic forgetting as an indicator of dataset membership.
    *   Strong empirical results: Demonstrates significantly improved performance compared to a baseline approach across multiple LLMs and datasets.
    *   Provides theoretical justification connecting CF and output shifts.
    *   Well-written and clear explanation of the method and experiments.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dependence on a "known non-member" validation set could be a limitation. Constructing such a set in practice could be challenging, especially if the potential training data is unknown. The paper acknowledges this through defining adversarial constraints and assumptions, however, this still remains an important consideration.
    *   The success of CatShift depends on fine-tuning. API costs and rate limits associated with fine-tuning commercial models might limit the applicability of the approach. It would be ideal to perform fewer queries.
    *   Fine-tuning introduces hyperparameters (learning rate, epochs, etc.) that might influence results and require careful tuning. The paper could discuss this more thoroughly.
    *   Doesn't evaluate defenses against CatShift.

*   **Potential Influence:** CatShift has the potential to influence the field significantly by:

    *   Shifting the focus of dataset inference research towards label-only settings, reflecting the realities of commercial LLM deployments.
    *   Providing a practical tool for dataset owners to protect their intellectual property.
    *   Stimulating further research into leveraging catastrophic forgetting for various tasks related to LLM security and privacy.

*   **Score Justification:**
I am assigning a score of **8**. While the concept of catastrophic forgetting is not inherently novel, its application to label-only dataset inference and the resulting framework, CatShift, are significant contributions. The practicality of the approach, addressing a pressing real-world problem, and the solid empirical results justify a high score. The practical dependency on the choice of parameters and the assumption of known non-members in the validation set slightly limit the applicability, but the paper is nonetheless a compelling and impactful contribution. It opens up a new and valuable avenue for dataset inference research and provides a practical tool for safeguarding proprietary data in the age of LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### Simple Yet Effective: Extracting Private Data Across Clients in Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06060v1)
- **Authors**: Yingqi Hu, Zhuo Zhang, Jingyuan Zhang, Lizhen Qu, Zenglin Xu
- **Abstract**: Federated fine-tuning of large language models (FedLLMs) presents a promising approach for achieving strong model performance while preserving data privacy in sensitive domains. However, the inherent memorization ability of LLMs makes them vulnerable to training data extraction attacks. To investigate this risk, we introduce simple yet effective extraction attack algorithms specifically designed for FedLLMs. In contrast to prior "verbatim" extraction attacks, which assume access to fragments from all training data, our approach operates under a more realistic threat model, where the attacker only has access to a single client's data and aims to extract previously unseen personally identifiable information (PII) from other clients. This requires leveraging contextual prefixes held by the attacker to generalize across clients. To evaluate the effectiveness of our approaches, we propose two rigorous metrics-coverage rate and efficiency-and extend a real-world legal dataset with PII annotations aligned with CPIS, GDPR, and CCPA standards, achieving 89.9% human-verified precision. Experimental results show that our method can extract up to 56.57% of victim-exclusive PII, with "Address," "Birthday," and "Name" being the most vulnerable categories. Our findings underscore the pressing need for robust defense strategies and contribute a new benchmark and evaluation framework for future research in privacy-preserving federated learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Simple Yet Effective: Extracting Private Data Across Clients in Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models" investigates the vulnerability of Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models (FedLLMs) to data extraction attacks. The authors propose a more realistic threat model where a malicious client leverages contextual prefixes from its own data to extract Personally Identifiable Information (PII) from other clients' data. They introduce three attack strategies: collecting all possible contextual prefixes, sampling high-frequency prefixes, and local fine-tuning on prefix-PII pairs. The effectiveness of the attacks is evaluated using coverage rate and efficiency metrics on a real-world legal dataset annotated with PII labels aligned with privacy regulations. The experiments reveal that the proposed methods can extract a significant amount of victim-exclusive PII, particularly "Address," "Birthday," and "Name" categories, highlighting a critical privacy risk in FedLLMs.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **more realistic threat model** for data extraction attacks in FedLLMs. Unlike prior works that assume access to fragments from all training data, this paper focuses on a single malicious client attempting to extract PII from other clients using only its own data and the shared model. This is a significant departure from previous assumptions and makes the research more relevant to practical scenarios. The proposed attack strategies, while "simple," are effective and represent a direct approach to exploiting memorization in FedLLMs under the new threat model. Extending the legal corpus with PII annotations with regulatory compliance in mind is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The findings of this paper are significant for several reasons.
    1.  It provides **concrete evidence** of the vulnerability of FedLLMs to PII extraction attacks, even under more constrained threat models.
    2.  It **highlights the specific PII categories** ("Address," "Birthday," "Name") that are most at risk, allowing for targeted development of defense mechanisms.
    3.  The proposed evaluation metrics (coverage rate and efficiency) provide a useful framework for future research in this area.
    4.  The developed real-world legal benchmark dataset by extending a legal corpus with PII annotations aligned with regulatory standards (CPIS, GDPR, CCPA) is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a clear and well-defined problem.
    *   The proposed attack strategies are straightforward to implement and effective.
    *   The evaluation is thorough and uses realistic data.
    *   The authors provide a detailed analysis of the results.
    *   The work underscores an important security and privacy challenge in federated learning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the threat model is more realistic, it could be further refined to consider defenses the victim might take to obfuscate the PII.
    *   The attack strategies, while effective, are relatively simple. The fine-tuning, for example, used random pairs of prefixes and PII, which could perhaps be made more intelligent.
    *   The paper focuses primarily on the attack side, and while it calls for defense strategies, it doesn't explore them in any detail. The exploration of defensive mechanisms is the natural next step.

*   **Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in privacy-preserving federated learning. It highlights the limitations of FedLLMs in sensitive domains and motivates the development of more robust defense mechanisms. The proposed evaluation framework can be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field. The benchmark dataset provides a valuable resource for evaluating the effectiveness of different attacks and defenses.

*   **Overall Score:**

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the vulnerability of FedLLMs to PII extraction attacks under a more realistic threat model. The novelty of the threat model, the significance of the findings, and the thorough evaluation make this a worthwhile contribution. While the attack strategies could be more sophisticated, and the paper does not discuss any defensive mechanisms, it effectively raises the alarm about the privacy risks in FedLLMs. The creation of a regulatory-compliant PII annotated real-world legal dataset also adds significant value. The paper’s impact is solid, motivating further research on defense strategies and a better understanding of the trade-offs between utility and privacy in federated learning.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Feedback Guidance of Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06085v1)
- **Authors**: Koulischer Felix, Handke Florian, Deleu Johannes, Demeester Thomas, Ambrogioni Luca
- **Abstract**: While Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) has become standard for improving sample fidelity in conditional diffusion models, it can harm diversity and induce memorization by applying constant guidance regardless of whether a particular sample needs correction. We propose FeedBack Guidance (FBG), which uses a state-dependent coefficient to self-regulate guidance amounts based on need. Our approach is derived from first principles by assuming the learned conditional distribution is linearly corrupted by the unconditional distribution, contrasting with CFG's implicit multiplicative assumption. Our scheme relies on feedback of its own predictions about the conditional signal informativeness to adapt guidance dynamically during inference, challenging the view of guidance as a fixed hyperparameter. The approach is benchmarked on ImageNet512x512, where it significantly outperforms Classifier-Free Guidance and is competitive to Limited Interval Guidance (LIG) while benefitting from a strong mathematical framework.
